| E11 | Support many AI agents, not just Claude Code. **The row was a bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-09-16).** **The finding that set the scope:** `SKILL.md` became a cross-agent format and `.agents/skills/` the directory Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, Cursor and GitHub Copilot agreed to read — so the install side was ALREADY multi-agent and every surface still said "Claude Code". Checked against each agent's own documentation on 2026-09-16, never from memory: a guessed skills path installs in silence and is never read. (1) **Scope: name the agents and wire the second hook.** `IDE_AGENTS` maps each directory to the agents that read it, and the `yad setup` menu, `yad doctor` and the docs all read that one table, so the list a user is shown cannot drift from the list that installs. `.cursor` and `.gemini` join as native folder-copy targets — redundant beside `.agents`, offered for a single-agent project. (2) **A fresh `yad setup` defaults to `.claude,.agents`**, which together cover every agent named; the skills are written twice, which is the honest cost. **The recovery fallback stays `.claude` alone and deliberately differs** — a project whose stamp is unreadable is restored to the minimum, not enrolled in a newer default that writes forty skill folders nobody asked for, on a path they did not choose to walk. A directory already present still beats both. (3) **Directory names only.** No `--agent cursor` alias, so the stamped value keeps meaning exactly what it always meant and no rename is staged. (4) **Cursor's `.cursor/hooks.json` is written with the same discipline as `.claude/settings.json`**: additive merge of one owned entry, a file that does not parse is never rewritten, a narrowed matcher is the team's, and neither file enters the `--push` staging allowlist. **The real gap was the ledger guard**, wired for one harness: `HOOK_ADAPTERS` makes it per-harness (file, event key, entry shape, tool matcher, command) and adds Cursor, whose `preToolUse` exit 2 is the deny `cli/hook.mjs` already described. Cursor's `afterFileEdit` is NOT used — it fires once the write has landed, so it could report but never refuse, and reporting is what the CI gate already does. **THE ONE THAT NEARLY SHIPPED, found by asking what Cursor does on a successful ALLOW:** its `preToolUse` is a PERMISSION hook, and its docs say invalid JSON or an off-schema response BLOCKS — empty stdout is invalid JSON, and the exit protocol prints nothing when it allows. Wiring the shared script straight into Cursor would have blocked EVERY file write in a verified project: fail-CLOSED on everything, the opposite of the design, and invisible to any test that only checks that a deny denies. So `yad hook ledger-guard --format cursor` answers `{"permission":"allow"}` / `{"permission":"deny","user_message":…,"agent_message":…}` on stdout (Cursor's schema is snake_case, and an off-schema response still BLOCKS — so a wrong field name refuses the write, passes every "a deny denies" test, and silently discards the text naming `yad gate open`, which is the only reason to speak at edit time), and `hooks/ledger-guard-cursor.sh` — installed ONLY for a project whose targets include `.cursor`, and taking no arguments, since the command string may never be split by a shell — converts every fail-open branch of the shared script into an explicit allow rather than the empty stdout that would block. The allow answer is a fixed literal because a malformed ALLOW is a block; the deny answer carries the reason, and an off-schema rejection there is still a block, which is what a deny wanted — both failure directions are safe, in opposite ways, on purpose. A deny exits 0, because the JSON is the authoritative answer and exit 0 is what tells Cursor to read it. **Two more silent-failure modes were closed rather than shipped, and both would have left `yad doctor` truthfully reporting an entry that never refuses anything:** Cursor's command is a RELATIVE, unquoted, variable-free path, because their docs say a project hook runs from the project root but not whether the string goes through a shell — an unexpanded `$CURSOR_PROJECT_DIR` or a literal quote is a command not found, which fails open; and Cursor does not document its `tool_input` field names, so `payloadPaths` matches any key NAMED like a path rather than guessing a vendor spelling — matching the KEY and never the value, since scanning values would refuse an ordinary edit to a document that merely quotes a ledger path, and a false deny is worse than a miss in a guard that fails open by design. `baseDirFor` reads every harness's project-root variable: reading only Claude's meant that under any other harness a relative payload path was anchored at the git toplevel, which in the documented multi-repo layout is a code repo, so the walk-up found no `hub.json` and allowed an edit it should have refused. `yad doctor` now NAMES the targets that can carry no guard, on the healthy line too — a project whose only target is `.agents` read "guard wired" while nothing local guarded anything. **No shape change, and forward-only:** adding values to `ideTargets` bumps nothing, but an OLDER yadflow reading a stamp naming `.cursor` or `.gemini` filters them as invalid and `needsRepair` rewrites the stamp without them — the skills stay installed, the targets are simply forgotten until the newer CLI runs again. **The guard script's bytes changed**, so every verified project sees `hooks/ledger-guard.sh` as `outdated` on its next `yad check` and refreshes it with `yad update`. **The review of the branch found five defects, all fixed before merge, and three are worth keeping:** (a) the deny field name was camelCase where Cursor's schema is snake_case — the block still happened, so nothing failed, and only the message was lost; (b) `hookMatcherFires` tested the matcher as a regex, but Cursor documents `*` as match-all and `new RegExp('*')` THROWS, so a correctly configured project was warned that its guard was dead; (c) adding `.cursor` to the supported list made `detectedIdeTargetStateFor` enrol every project that merely keeps Cursor RULES in `.cursor/` — no stamp, so the fallback ran, and the next `yad check --fix` would have written 38 skill folders and a hooks.json for a team that never asked. Detection now requires the INSTALL CONTAINER (`<ide>/skills/`, or `.opencode/commands/`), not the bare directory, which is the question it was always trying to answer. **A second review of the fixes found three more:** `yad doctor` checked only the shared script, so a project that lost `hooks/ledger-guard-cursor.sh` — the file Cursor's entry actually names — got a green line while `yad check` called it `new`; and the shipped `ledger-guard.sh` header plus `docs/CLI.md` still told a reader that Cursor is wired to the shared script directly, which is the fail-closed bug itself, written down as instructions. **A third, deep review of the whole branch found fifteen more, and the pattern in nearly all of them is the one this row exists to fix — a guard that reports healthy while doing nothing.** The ones worth remembering: a wired script that lost its EXECUTE BIT read as `ok` because status compared bytes only and `chmod` lives inside `apply()`, so the harness ran a command that could not start and every edit was permitted (fixed in `fileAction`, for every exec-wired script, not just this one); the wrapper resolved its own directory with a bare `cd`, and since the Cursor entry is a RELATIVE path an exported `CDPATH` — an ordinary thing in a login profile — silently pointed it at another tree; it recognised the verdict by PREFIX, so `yad`'s own stdout failure banner in front of the JSON turned a deny into an allow; `--format=cursor` (the equals form) fell through the parser to the positionals, selecting the exit protocol, which under Cursor BLOCKS every write; `mergeHookSettings` rebuilt — i.e. deleted — a `hooks.preToolUse` that was not a list, in a co-owned file on the no-backup path; requiring an install container DROPPED `.claude` from a project whose skills live in `.agents/` but whose `.claude/settings.json` was armed, and `needsRepair` wrote the loss into the stamp; `baseDirFor` never read the payload's own `cwd` and preferred the git toplevel to the process cwd, so a Product in a subdirectory of its repo allowed the edit; the guard never read `tool_name`, so a widened matcher made it refuse READS of `state.json`, which `yad status` and the review gate do routinely; and `yad doctor` said `ok — agent ledger guard wired` for a project where every target was one no harness can hook. Two process findings came with them: the release check's own fresh install silently doubled to two directories and stopped exercising the single-target shape every existing project upgrades from, and five doc surfaces — including `skills/yad-checks/SKILL.md`, which is INSTALLED INTO USER PROJECTS — still told a reader to wire Cursor to the shared script, which is the fail-closed bug written down as instructions. **`--ide-targets` came out of running an upgrade rather than reading the diff:** `ask` returns its default non-interactively and `ideTargets` was reachable only programmatically, so a scripted `yad setup` had no way to decline a default that had just become two directories. **The other install targets were NOT examined for a hook protocol** — the scope closed at Cursor — so "no adapter" means "not wired", never "checked and found wanting". **The four the audit left open are closed too.** (1) Dropping a target now UNWIRES it: `orphanHookActions` takes our entry out of that harness's settings file (leaving the team's, and leaving a `version` we cannot prove we wrote) and removes a wrapper no remaining target needs — otherwise the entry kept firing while every check here, being keyed on the current targets, had stopped looking at it. (2) `yad doctor` now names a hand-wired command that would refuse EVERY write on a permission-hook harness, reported BEFORE the `not wired` branch whose remedy is wrong there (`check --fix` would add ours beside theirs and leave the blocking one in place); it is the one place a command we did not write is judged, it only reports, and it fires only on a command naming our own scripts. (3) `uri`/`url` keys are read when — and only when — the value is a `file://` URL, decoded; an http URL stays ignored, because claiming one would be a false deny. (4) The hook's hot path no longer loads the whole CLI: every command module moved behind one dynamic import in `bin/commands.mjs`, and `yad hook ledger-guard` short-circuits above it, measured at **37ms per call against ~80ms** — paid on every file-editing tool call an agent makes. **Not here:** detecting which agents are actually installed (E50), per-profile skill defaults (E51), agents talking to the engine over MCP (E2), Windows behaviour (Wave 3's own row), and exercising the Cursor wiring against a live Cursor session — it follows Cursor's published protocol and is stated as unverified in the README and `check-gates.md` rather than claimed **Shipped (#249).** | M | E6 |
| E62 | **Remove the roster completely** — 399 references, 20 files. **The row was a bare title, so five decisions were the user's (2026-09-16).** **The finding that set the scope:** the roster was not one feature but six jobs — it decided whether a team gate passes (1 owner + `defaultReviewers` reviewers + a domain owner per touched repo, every one a ROLE read from the roster), named who wrote a record (`by`), requested PR reviewers, fed the author allowlist of `verified-commits.sh`, seeded `yad usage`, and carried per-repo owners through `repos.json` `domain_owners`. The first job is the one that could not simply be deleted: with no roster nobody holds `owner`, so every team gate would fail forever, and E7's count may not hold a gate until E72 caps it. (1) **Until E72, a team gate needs ONE approval from someone other than the author.** That is E7's `base`, enforced; the risk step stays advisory and is still printed as the shortfall. It is the only number that cannot lock a small team out without a cap. It is weaker than the role rule on a contract gate, and that is acceptable ONLY because nothing between E62 and E72 is released: publishing happens from `release` and `next` alone, and the user will not release before Waves 3 and 3.5 are done — so no user ever runs the in-between rule. Do not push `next` mid-wave. The golden's pass/fail stays the same on all nine gates (each has at least two approvers); only the `rule` label moves, and the snapshot is updated in the same PR. `defaultReviewers` retires with the role rule, and so does `isEscalated`'s `stories-review` clause; `touchedDomains` stays for PR labels. `requireEngagement` stays — it is not about people. (2) **Data already on disk is left alone and `yad doctor` warns it is unused** — the `roster` key, `repos.json` `domain_owners`, `verified_authors` and a generated `.sdlc/verified-authors` (`people:roster-unused`, `people:domain-owners-unused`, `people:verified-authors-unused`). The `role`/`domain` fields on old approvals are left too, but not warned about: they are history on every existing epic, and a warning per record would be noise that changes the golden `epics` section. Nothing writes any of it; nothing deletes it. **One read survives, decided by the user after review (2026-09-16):** the roster's name → login pairs (`legacyLogins`) are read to recognise the person an older approval or comment names, and for nothing else — see finding (a). No shape change and no migration; a later major may delete them. `yad migrate`'s shape-3 step that rewrites the roster key keeps working, so a v3 project can still upgrade through it. (3) **The author allowlist half of `verified-commits.sh` is deleted**, as the appendix says; the platform signature check stays. Write access to the repo is the allowlist. `yad check --fix` stops generating `.sdlc/verified-authors`. The risk, stated: in a user's repo, once the refreshed script lands, a Verified commit from an email nobody listed now passes CI. (4) **A record's `by` is the platform login** — asked of `gh`/`glab` once per command, recorded as the handle checkpoints already use — **else git `user.name`** when there is no platform, no network or no login. (5) **The smaller jobs, decided with the row:** `yad roster` is removed and prints where the people model went, exiting non-zero; `yad setup` asks for no people (it had no scripted roster flag to keep — the roster section was interactive only, and `--name`/`--email`/`--roles` belonged to `yad roster`); PRs request no reviewers (E68 suggests them from history); `yad usage` builds its people from activity alone and loses `dormant`, `off-roster` and `reviewer-not-reviewing`, all of which needed a stored list. **What the work found, beyond the decisions.** (a) **An upgrade hole, and a first fix that was wrong twice.** An older approval names the person as the roster did (`alice`) while the platform reports the login (`al`), so the first sync after an upgrade treated every such approval as new and bound it to TODAY's content — the #156 hole again. The first fix matched older records by PR and submission time and, on GitLab (no submission time), by list order, preferring "a fingerprint that is not today's". Two reviews broke it. By order, two people's fingerprints swapped, and alice's approval of old text passed a gate once carol withdrew; on a closed step a person's history was deleted or counted twice. And "not today's" was the wrong test of stale: every approval a released yadflow wrote carries a pre-shape-9 fingerprint form the gate still accepts, so a live record read as stale and a stale approval passed. **Now:** (1) the roster's name → login pairs make the match exact (decision 2's one read); (2) without a roster, a record is continued only when nothing else could be it — the same submission time on GitHub, or one unmatched approval against one unmatched older approver of the PR on GitLab; (3) stale means outside `acceptedHashes`, and when one person's records disagree the stale one is kept; (4) still ambiguous, a closed step adds nothing and an open step binds the approval to a stale fingerprint from those records, so it must be given again on a new review. Comment rounds use the same pairs, so the first sync opens no extra round. **A third review broke that version too, in narrower ways, now fixed:** an older record marked `unverified` already names a login, and translating it through a roster name it collided with passed a gate on an outsider's old approval; the GitLab one-to-one guess on a CLOSED step handed one person's history to another, so a closed step now continues a record only on an exact submission time; and approvals were matched one at a time, so the result depended on the platform's list order and a re-sync was not byte-identical — all approvals are now judged against the same older set, and two that would continue one record continue neither. **The two limits the PR first listed, addressed after it opened.** (i) *A roster name two logins share* is no longer matched by name: a record under it is keyed apart from every login, so it is continued only as a record no name can place (an exact submission time, or an open step's one-to-one); otherwise an open step must be re-approved and a closed step keeps it as history. `yad doctor` warns `people:roster-ambiguous`. A name equal to ANOTHER entry's login needed nothing — it was already exact, because an older record under a name was written from that entry and an unlisted login was written `unverified` — so the warning no longer claims it. (ii) *`yad gate open` replaced the review pointer without stamping the old PR on approvals that predate PR provenance* (only `gateCi` did), so the next sync stamped the NEW number, and a re-approval on a GitLab re-opened review stayed stale for good. It now stamps before replacing, as `gateCi` does. **Still not fixable here, handed to E64:** a project whose roster was DELETED before its first post-upgrade sync can only match older records that nothing else could be (fail-closed: re-approve on a new review), and a pointer an OLDER release already moved without stamping stays stale until the review is opened again. The permanent fix is E64's: record the platform login on every approval while the roster still exists, so no later sync needs the roster at all. **A second review round and an upgrade simulation run with the OLDER release (2026-09-16) found more, all fixed:** records under a shared name held two people in one group, so continuing one deleted or restamped the other — the key now carries the submission time, and only the logins the roster gives that name can continue it; `yad gate open` read the PR number from the FIRST number in the URL (`acme/2048/pull/9` → #2048, pre-existing) and, for a URL with no number, skipped the stamp; `people:roster-ambiguous` advised renaming an entry, which hands the older records to whoever keeps the name — a rename then passed a gate on old content in the simulation, so on GitHub an exact submission time now beats the name table, and the hint says to leave the roster alone (GitLab has no submission time, so a rename there can still misattribute; the docs say not to); comment rounds follow the same shared-name rule; `yad usage` reads older records through the same name table, because a roster name can be another person's login and the two landed on one row; and `yad doctor` warns `people:allowlist-gate-stale` for an older `verified-commits.sh` that still enforces the author list — a local-ledger Product's CI files are not refreshed by `yad check --fix`. **Correction to decision 2:** a record the first sync CONTINUES is rewritten under the login and drops `role`/`domain`/`unverified` — keeping `role` on a login-named record would make the next sync read it as an older record and look the login up as a roster name, possibly another person's; the dated `reviews/*--approved.md` keeps the roles. Records nothing continues are left exactly as they were. Each case is a test, and each fix was mutation-checked. (b) **Setup's solo/team default came from the roster's size.** With no mode recorded, an existing Product now defaults to TEAM (the recorded `team_size` decides when there is one): solo waives every approval, and a scripted re-run of an old Product must not fall into it. (c) **`yad usage` would have split people** whose git name differs from their login; a GitHub/GitLab noreply commit address carries the login, so those commits join that login's approvals (case-insensitively — logins are), and the report says others may appear twice. `no-review-participation` is raised only on a row known by login: a bare git-name row cannot see its person's approvals, so "never reviews" would be false. (d) **`isEscalated` lost its `stories-review` clause**, but `touchedDomains` asks for the stories' repos first and on its own, so the stories review PR is still labelled with every touched repo. (e) **`risk-route.sh` and `hub-route.sh`** printed the role rule too and now print the gate's own sum; `hub-route.sh` had no tests and gained them. (f) **The approval-count wording moved:** `rule` is `count`; the surfaces say `count: … — base enforced, risk step advisory — N short`, and the review-PR body `Approvals needed: 1 (enforced) · full count …`. (g) **Smaller review findings, fixed:** an approval record naming nobody counted as one person and could pass a team gate alone — it now counts as nobody, in the predicate and in `gate status`; `gh api user` asked github.com even for a GitHub Enterprise Product, so the login lookup passes the repo's host, and GitHub assignees go back to `@me`, which `gh` resolves on the right host; a GitLab login may start with `_`; `yad doctor` says nothing about an empty `roster` value. (h) **The "not the author" half is the platform's, not the engine's:** GitHub never allows self-approval, GitLab only when the project's approval settings say so, and on a local ledger nothing checks it — the docs now say so instead of claiming it is guaranteed. **Golden:** pass/fail, `missing` and `staleDropped` are unchanged on all nine frozen gates; six `rule` labels moved from `base`/`escalated`/`per-repo` to `count`. **Left on purpose:** this repo's own `.sdlc/hub.json` still carries a roster and `repos.json` names owners, so `yad doctor` warns on it (decision 2); the bytes of the wired `verified-commits.sh` and `risk-route.sh`, the CI files that run verified-commits and the code-repo PR/MR templates changed, so every wired project reads them `outdated` until `yad update`; `createPr` keeps its `reviewers` parameter for E68. **Not here:** the capacity cap and enforcing the risk step (E71/E72), recording platform evidence on approvals beyond the login (E64), reviewer suggestions and CODEOWNERS (E68) **Shipped (#250).** | L | E13 |
| E64 | Approvals record platform evidence, not a config role. **The row was a near-bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-09-16).** **Two findings set the scope.** (a) GitLab's approvals API returns `approved_at` for each approver, and `readPrGitLab` threw it away — E62's matcher, `login-roster.md` and the E62 row all assumed GitLab has no submission time. Read when present, GitLab gets the same exact-time match GitHub has; an old self-hosted GitLab that does not send it keeps today's behaviour. (b) `yad migrate` cannot stamp the ledger: `approvals.json` and `comments.json` are top-level lists, which `planMigration` never rewrites, and on a verified Product they are CI's alone. So the stamp rides the gate write, beside `stampLegacyPr`. (1) **The stamp rewrites an older record under its login and drops `role`/`domain`/`unverified`** — exactly what E62 does to a record it continues, for the reason E62's correction gives. One record per person per step; where one person's older records disagree, the stale one (outside `acceptedHashes`) is kept. The dated `reviews/*--approved.md` keeps the roles. A name two logins share, a name with no login and an `unverified` record are left alone and still fail closed. Comment records get the same. (2) **It runs on every gate write, over every step of the epic, closed ones included** — `yad gate sync` on the writer path and `yad gate ci` — **and the CI sweep also visits an epic that still holds older records when it has no open review**, because the sweep's job list skips passed steps and would never reach an all-closed epic. `yad doctor`'s `people:roster-unused` counts the older records still under roster names: while any remain it says how they get stamped; at zero it says the roster is safe to delete. (3) **A synced approval records, as audit only: a real `approvedAt` time on both platforms, and on GitHub the review's `commit`, `url` and node `id`.** A GitLab record carries no commit — the MR head is not the approval's commit. The gate still decides on the login and the fingerprint; no numeric user id and no push-access claim are stored (Part 3: compute claims about people, do not store them). (4) **Hand-written (local-ledger) approvals are unchanged**; the docs say plainly they carry no evidence and, with no `artifactHash`, are never revoked when the artifact changes — a gap that predates E64 and gets its own row. **No shape change:** E62 already writes records this way, and an older release ignores optional keys rather than misreading them (the shape-10 test; E18's `closed` key is the precedent). **Build trap:** every GitLab bridge record on disk holds a date-only `approvedAt`, and `"2026-09-15T10:00:00Z" > "2026-09-15"` as text — so a date-only value must read as "time unknown", or the first sync re-binds every same-day GitLab approval to today's content (#156). **Handed over from E62:** while an older roster still exists, record the platform login on every older approval and comment record (the roster's name → login pairs, `legacyLogins`), so no later sync depends on the roster being kept — today a roster deleted before the first post-upgrade sync can only fail closed. **What the build found.** (a) *A stamp without the platform could lock in a wrong name.* E62 lets an exact GitHub submission time beat the name table (a renamed shared name); a record stamped onto a login stopped being "older" and lost that. A stamped approval therefore keeps its old name in `rosterName`, and the exact-time rule matches it as it matched a role-bearing record. The evidence keys are recorded as `commit`, `url` and `reviewId`. (b) *The wired sweep never runs a bare `yad gate ci`*: it calls `--branch … --merged` per recently merged review, and a run that advances nothing did not commit. So decision 2's "sweep" is every `--merged` run, and the stamp is a reason to commit in its own right, like the shape-8 move; a verified Product with no merged review in the window is stamped on its next merge. (c) *A GitLab record written before E64 holds a date*, so the no-roster one-to-one rule must still apply when the approval now carries `approved_at`, and such a record takes the platform's time once without taking today's fingerprint. (d) A Product with no platform never needs the stamp (nothing matches logins), and doctor says the roster can go. **An adversarial review and an upgrade simulation built with v3.18.1 and pre-E64 main (2026-09-17) found more, all fixed:** the stamp merged two people a renamed roster gives one name when GitLab had synced them the same day (both records held the same date), and a gate passed on one's approval of old content — records are now merged only when every one holds the same submission TIME and `pr`, so on GitLab a person's role records usually stay for the roster to match on their review's next sync; one malformed record in any epic threw inside the `gate ci` stamp and `yad doctor`, stopping every merge; the `gate ci` stamp ran before the jobs, so a job that threw left other epics stamped but uncommitted — it now runs after them, skips epics the run synced or failed, and treats an uncommitted `reviews/` file as dirty so it is never committed; `yad gate sync` discarded the stamp on an epic with no review PR on file, so doctor's advice repeated for ever; `yad usage` lost the login on a stamped record. Each case is a test, and each guard was mutation-checked. **A second review of those fixes found one more, fixed:** an older record whose `artifact` is empty or names a folder, in the merged review's OWN epic, threw inside `gateSync` and stopped that merge — an empty artifact is now unreadable, a group whose live fingerprints cannot be computed is left as it is, and a stamp failure is named while the sync goes on; the stamp also moved after the `pr` backfill, so one review's role records merge on the first write, and a stamped comment keeps `rosterName`. **Two holes the simulation found in E62's matcher, not caused by E64, handed to the next `fix(gate)` PR (the user chose a separate PR, 2026-09-17):** on GitHub, when two people once shared a roster name and BOTH approved content that later changed, (i) with the roster kept after one entry was renamed, one person's exact time claims the other's name group and `repOf` hands back the first stale record, so a later time reads as a newer review and both approvals re-bind to today's content; (ii) with the roster deleted, one person claims the name group by exact time and the other claims the same group again through the orphan path (orphans are not filtered by `replaced`, and `claims` counts only orphan picks). Both passed the gate on changed content in the simulation; the fix is to claim records per review, not per name group. **Fixed in that follow-up PR (`fix/e62-matcher-per-review`, 2026-09-17):** an exact submission time now continues only the older records holding that time, whether or not the name also matches, and a name group continued by time is no longer an orphan; both GitHub cases fail closed in either platform order, in tests and in the simulation. A review of that change found the delete was still per name group — on a closed step, x's review deleted y's history under the shared name (pre-existing) — so records are now claimed per review everywhere: a match by name continues the records holding the approval's own time when there are any, and a closed step keeps a record no review continued. A fourth review confirmed the other side of that choice, and the user chose to keep it (2026-09-17): the records cannot tell two people who once shared a name from one person whose role records sit at two different times, so in the rarer second case a closed step lists that person twice and `gate status` shows one approver more; nothing advances, and it is documented in `login-roster.md`. Still a limit, as documented: on GitLab, a renamed shared name with the roster kept can put an older approval on the wrong person, because pre-E64 GitLab records hold only a date. Also seen, smaller: on a closed GitLab step where one person holds a stale and a live record written on different days, keeping the roster merges them to the stale one while deleting it keeps both, so `gate status` wording depends on when the roster goes **Shipped (#251).** | M | E62 |
| E65 | Directory → risk-level map, one per code repo. No names in it. **The row was a bare title, so the decisions were the user's (2026-09-17).** It began as "risk-tag → path map"; the user changed what it maps. **Two findings set the scope.** (a) A path → tag map already existed, hard-coded: `RISK_PATTERNS` in `cli/walkthrough.mjs` guesses `auth` / `payments` / `contract` from file NAMES to order the pair-review walkthrough. Names are exactly what breaks across naming habits and project types. (b) Code-repo CI never checks out the Product — `contract-check.sh` reaches it only when it happens to be on disk — so a Product-wide map could not be relied on where PRs are checked. (1) **Generic levels, not topic tags.** An entry says `high`, `medium` or `low`. `high` is today's high tier (+1). `medium` adds nothing and is reported only, so E7's numbers do not move. `low` adds nothing. **`contract` is not a level and never appears in the map**: the contract surface keeps its own lock, `contract-check.sh`, `Contract-Change` trailer and +2. **Two vocabularies now exist, and they meet in E66:** a Shape step's `risk_tags` stay `contract` / `auth` / `payments` (`RISK_TIERS`), and `gateRuleFor` still ignores any other tag — `risk_tags: ['high']` adds nothing. A `high` directory is what maps to the high tier. (2) **One file per code repo, `.sdlc/risk-map`**, changed only through that repo's PRs. It is the team's file, not a wired one: it is never in `REPO_WIRING` or `.sdlc/managed.json`, so `yad update` never offers to overwrite it. (3) **One line per directory, readable by bash with no tools:** a `# yad-risk-map v1` header, then `<dir>/ <level> <guessed\|confirmed> # <reason>`. Fields split on spaces and tabs only, so a path holds neither; everything from the first ` #` is a comment and is stripped explicitly; `./` means the files at the repo root; the deepest listed directory above a file decides its level. A level may be `unset` — listed, not yet classified. No names: the check and `yad doctor` warn on an `@login`. (4) **The guess comes from what the code does, not from names, and only when drafting.** `yad risk-map draft <repo>` (deterministic) adds an `unset` line for every directory no line covers — on a new map, each top-level directory and `./` when the root holds files — and never changes an entry already there. The `yad-connect-repos` skill then has the AI agent read the code — on connect and on `yad repo refresh`, which already read the whole repo for the code map — and fill each `unset` entry with a level, a one-line reason taken from the code, and `guessed`. It splits a folder one level deeper where it holds parts of different risk, and follows a written rubric (moving money, login / sessions / secrets, deleting data, CI and deploy, personal data → `high`). A `confirmed` entry is never rewritten; a change of meaning is printed as a suggestion. With no AI agent, entries stay `unset` for a person. A person confirms by changing `guessed` to `confirmed` in a PR; the commit records who, and no name enters the file. (5) **A check on every PR: warnings only, no AI, the same answer every run.** `checks/risk-map-check.sh`, in both CI templates, warns about a changed file no entry covers (naming the directory to add), an entry whose directory holds no file (dead), a touched entry still `guessed` or `unset`, a line it cannot read, and a PR that edits the map itself (that changes how much review later PRs need). It never fails the build. A repo with no map gets one note. `yad risk-map check` and `yad doctor` (for each connected repo on disk) say the same. (6) **Nothing counts it yet** — that is E66, which reads the map from the BASE branch so a PR cannot lower its own count. A directory with no entry is warned about, never counted as `high`. **No shape change:** a new optional file with its own version line. **Cost:** the new check and CI job change wired bytes, so every wired repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`. **One rubric line is not about what the code does:** `.sdlc/` is `high` because it HOLDS the map, so a change to it decides how much review every later change needs (the user's decision, 2026-09-17). The cost is accepted: a `yad update` PR rewrites that folder and asks for the extra approver too. **Not here:** counting (E66), the history query (E67), and the pair-review walkthrough, which keeps `RISK_PATTERNS`. **Where it lives, on a verified Product:** the map is a code-repo file, not a Product ledger file, so `ledger-guard` never matches it and a person commits it through a PR there. **The rules are written twice** — `cli/riskmap.mjs` for `yad risk-map` and `yad doctor`, and awk inside `checks/risk-map-check.sh`, because a code repo's CI may have no Node — and a parity test runs both over the same repos and compares every finding in order. **What the build found.** Two review rounds, each fix a test, the load-bearing ones mutation-checked. (a) `draft` wrote a line for a directory no line can hold (`app/[slug]/`, `my docs/`, `#tmp/`), which the parser rejected, so every later draft added it again — now a parent line covers such a directory, and a top-level one is named as unwritable (a newline or CR in a name included). (b) A file list over 1 MiB failed `spawnSync`'s default buffer and read as "not a git repo"; the list also skips `run()`'s trim, which cut a leading space off the first path. (c) git quotes a path holding `"`, `\` or a tab even with `core.quotePath=false`, so the bash check reads NUL-separated lists. (d) An empty or unparseable `repos.json` fell back to treating the Product as the code repo and wrote a map into it; now only a missing registry means "run inside a code repo". (e) A PR deleting the map read as "no map"; it warns `map-edited`, among many deletions and for a rename away. (f) The two twins split on `v0` headers, form feeds and non-breaking spaces, and `@types/` read as a name — the version is compared as text, only spaces and tabs are whitespace, and a word ending in `/` is a directory (`@org/team` is still a name). (g) **The check measures the change with three dots** (from where the branch left its base), unlike every blocking gate, which uses two: with two, commits the base gained since were blamed on this change. It is safe here because the check only warns about what the change touches; the blocking gates keep two dots. Round one's switch then exited 128 on a shallow clone with no merge base, so the base now counts only when it shares history with HEAD. **Known limits, stated:** a path holding a newline reads differently in the bash check than in `yad risk-map check` (macOS awk cannot split on NUL), and so does a path whose bytes are not valid UTF-8 (the check compares bytes, the CLI decodes a bad byte as U+FFFD); a map renamed to another case is silent on a case-insensitive disk; the CLI also sees untracked files while CI sees tracked ones, so a directory holding only new files can differ locally; the parity test has run on macOS awk only — the repo's own CI on Ubuntu is its first mawk run **Shipped (#253).** | M | E62 |
| E66 | Escalate by count. **Reads E65's map:** a PR touching a `high` directory adds the high step (+1), read from `.sdlc/risk-map` on the BASE branch; `medium` and `low` add nothing; the largest step wins, as in E7. **A `guessed` level counts, exactly as a `confirmed` one does (the user's decision, 2026-09-17):** a level only ever RAISES the number of approvals, so an AI's guess can ask for one more human and can never let a gate pass on its own (Part 8 rule 3 holds). Leaving a guess uncounted would have made the safest state — nobody has confirmed the levels yet — the one that asks for the least review. **What the build settled, from the code.** (1) **Build only.** The Shape gate reviews Product files, never a code-repo diff, so it cannot see the map: `gateRuleFor`, `hub-route.sh`, `gate status`, `gate sync` and the review-PR body are unchanged, and a step's `risk_tags` stay its only risk source. **The two vocabularies meet in one printed sum:** a map `high` is the step and tier an `auth` / `payments` tag gets (`LEVEL_STEP`, `cli/riskmap.mjs`), pinned by a test, so both print `base 1 + high risk 1`; `risk_tags: ['high']` still adds nothing (E65's pin kept). (2) **Reported, not enforced** — only the base of one approver holds a merge until E72, as E7 set. (3) **The base TIP's map**, read with `git show <base>:.sdlc/risk-map`, no Node; only a real file there is a map (a symlink or folder is not read). The change is the same three-dot range as E65's check under `LC_ALL=C`, but with **no diff filter and `--no-renames`**: a deleted file and a file moved away count where they were, because deleting code in a `high` directory is a `high` change. Deleting or lowering the map in the PR changes nothing; a map the PR adds counts from its merge on. An `unset` line under a `high` one takes its files out of the count (the deepest line decides, E65), and is warned about. (4) **Body vs map: the larger step wins**, so the body can raise and never lower; when the map raises a body saying `low`, the output names the `high` directories and says the two disagree. A map that cannot be read (no base, no shared history, a newer version) is said as "not counted — unknown, not zero", and the body alone counts; nothing is guessed. **Where it prints — nothing is written, no file shape moves:** `checks/risk-map-check.sh` prints a `COUNT [risk-map]:` line on every PR (the map's half; it has no body); `checks/risk-route.sh <body> [<base>]`, run in the code repo on the PR's branch, joins body and map by calling the check's new `--level` mode (machine lines), so the rules are still written twice, not three times — one awk program in the check now serves both the warnings and the count, and `changeLevel` is its JS twin with a parity test; `yad open-pr` prints the joined count once the PR is open. `yad doctor` prints none — a count belongs to one change. **Three decisions were the user's (2026-09-18):** `yad open-pr` prints the count and does NOT rewrite the body's `Risk level` (the body stays the author's judgment); the ship record's `risk` stays the body's level, not the map-raised one (the count is derived live, as E7 and E67 hold); risk-route is NOT a CI job — the skill calls it, and the CI risk-map job carries the map's half. **Costs, stated:** `risk-map-check.sh` and `risk-route.sh` change wired bytes, so every wired repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`; and, as E65 accepted, a `yad update` PR rewrites `.sdlc/`, which the rubric marks `high`, so it now prints one more approver. **Known limits:** the count runs from the PR's own checkout of `checks/`, so a PR can edit the script that counts it — true of every check, and harmless while the count is only reported, but E72 must answer it before the count holds a merge; `yad open-pr` reads `origin/<base>` as this clone last fetched it; `risk-route.sh` run on the base branch sees an empty change and says so; a path holding a newline is split in two by the bash count (E65's limit, now reaching the count), which can only raise it. **What the review found, each fixed with a test and mutation-checked:** `git ls-tree` reads its path from the current folder, so `risk-map-check.sh` or `risk-route.sh` run from a subfolder found no map and quietly counted zero (now `--full-tree` in both twins, and the check moves to the repo root first, so its E65 warnings — which read the map from the current folder — agree with the count); inside `$(...)` bash does not stop on a failing command, so a failing `git show` in the CI count read as an empty map, a false zero (now each git step of the count — `ls-tree`, `show`, `diff` — is guarded and says "not counted"; a third round found the `ls-tree` one still read a failure as "no map"); a `risk-route.sh` beside an E65 `risk-map-check.sh` (one updated, one customised and kept) read the old script's output as "nothing is high" (now "not counted"); and, older than E66 but reached more often by it, `risk-route.sh` and `hub-route.sh` exited 1 on a Domains line ending in a comma. **Left, older than E66:** if git itself fails in the E65 warnings half, the script can exit with git's code **Shipped (#255).** | S | E65 |
| E67 | Escalate by proven history — a live git query at gate time, nothing stored. **Since E65 the question is per directory:** an approval from someone who committed in the last 30 days to the `high` directories the change touches, not to a named topic. **The row was a near-bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-09-20):** (1) a person is their git NAME plus `(@login)` only when the commit address is a platform `noreply` one (`loginFromEmail`) — no platform API call, so it works in a code repo's CI, and **no e-mail address is ever printed**; (2) it is a REPORTED ask with a shortfall, not only a hint — `yad-engineer-review` compares the recorded approvers; (3) it prints in E66's three places (the CI check, `risk-route.sh`, `yad open-pr`); (4) with nobody to name it says so plainly and the count stands — a new or dormant directory can never make a change unreviewable. **What the build settled.** **Build only, like E66** (the Shape gate has no code history). The history is the BASE branch's (`git log <base> --since='30 days ago' --no-merges`), so a change cannot add its own, and **the change's own authors are left out** — an approval has to come from someone else. **GIT APPLIES THE COVER RULE, and no file name is ever read back** — the decision the fourth review round forced, and the one that matters most here. One query runs per `high` directory, in map order, carrying the pathspecs that say what the map means by it: the directory, minus every listed directory below it whatever its level (the deepest line decides, and each of those answers for itself in its own query, because an exclude beats a later include and they cannot share one). So with `src/payments/ high` and `src/payments/legacy/ low`, work in `legacy/` is not payments history. `./` is the files AT the root (`:(glob)*`; `*` never spans `/`). Git returns author records only, and both twins ask it the same way. A robot (`…[bot]`) is never listed: an approval cannot come from one, and `[` also makes the login rule reject it, so without the rule it would be printed by name as if it could approve. **A shallow clone says "not read", never "nobody"** — it holds only the newest commits, and reading that as "nobody has worked here" would DROP the ask instead of raising it, the same class as E66's false zero. **The third answer the skill needs:** an approval records a platform login while git records a name, so when the named people have no login the review says **could not confirm**, never "short". **Rule 3 move:** `loginFromEmail` now lives in `cli/riskmap.mjs` (the awk twin derives the same login, and a test compares them) and `cli/usage.mjs` re-exports it. **Nothing is stored** (E7's stance) and no file shape moves; `git log --format` separates a name from an address with `\x1f`, never NUL, because an awk that reads C strings would cut the line. **Every pathspec built from a map NAME is `:(literal)`** — `./` is the exception, since it names no directory and becomes `:(glob)*` (the fifth round's one finding): a map may legally list a directory whose name starts with `:`, and git reads that as pathspec MAGIC — `:weird/` would answer about `weird/`, and `:/` about the whole repo. The map's own name must describe a directory to git, never tell git what to do. **FOUR review rounds found EIGHT bugs of one class — something unreadable or odd read as "nobody" or as the WRONG person — and the fourth round is why the design changed.** In order: a quoted path (a non-ASCII byte, a `"`, a `\` or a tab) matched no map line, so its author dropped out, and a directory whose only recent work used such a path read as "nobody"; a rename was followed by one twin and not the other; a check older than E67 printed nothing about history and read as "nobody"; an awk line rebuilt with `$1 = ""` squashed a name's own spacing, printing one person three ways; a file NAME holding a newline split into two paths, so its second half read as work in the `high` directory and named someone who was never there; git simplified a path-filtered log and hid a side branch whose merge kept the other side; the newline guard then stripped the separator from EVERY path line rather than a commit's first, rewriting a later odd name into another file; and the twins refused on different inputs, because one filtered the log by directory and the other read all of it. **The user chose to delete the class rather than patch a ninth time (2026-09-20):** every one of those came from reading a list of file names back out of git and matching it against map lines, so the twins stopped doing that. `--full-history` and `--no-renames` survive; the marker scheme, the NUL-to-newline dance, the refusal for a newline in a name and the awk cover pass over the log are all gone with it. **The test lesson, worth more than the fixes:** the parity test hand-built a copy of the reader's git command, so a flag added to one twin only sailed straight through it — it now calls the real reader (`recentAuthorsFor`). **Known limits:** git's date-limited walk stops at the first commit older than the window on a chain, so out-of-order commit dates can hide people behind it — it under-lists, so every name printed is a real one; a person with two commit addresses is two rows; `--since` reads the COMMITTER date, so a rebase or squash refreshes it. **One list, not one per directory:** with two `high` directories the people are printed as one list — someone who has worked in ANY of them meets the ask, and nothing records who worked where. Per-directory attribution would change the `WHO` line, all three printers and the parity test, and nothing today needs it. **Boundary with E68:** this row is the `high` directories of one change, 30 days, an ask the review reports; E68 is a general reviewer SUGGESTION over any directory, with CODEOWNERS as a hint **Shipped (#256).** | S | E65 |
| E71 | Count active people live, three windows, capacity scaled to pace. **The row was a bare title, so the decisions were the user's (2026-09-21).** **The safety direction is INVERTED from the two rows before it, and that is the whole shape of this one.** E66 and E67 had to stop an unreadable input reading as "nobody", because that DROPS an ask and under-asks for review. Here a low `active` LOWERS E72's cap and weakens every gate, so the dangerous answer is a partial read that looks like "few people". Part 3 says it in one line: when counting capacity, err towards MORE people. So `cli/people.mjs` has ONE rule — **if a source cannot be read the answer is `null`, never a number** — and not a floor either, because a floor on `active` is a CEILING on the cap, the same mistake wearing the other hat. **Why a fresh reader and not `yad usage`, which already derives a people view (the user asked this explicitly, and the code answered it):** all four of its degradations run the wrong way for a gate — `repoCommitEvents` skips a repo git cannot read, `deriveEvents` reads a corrupt `repos.json` as "no repos" (the bug E65 fixed in `targets()` with `readJSONStrict`), `readLedger` skips a corrupt approvals file, and there is no shallow-clone check. Correct for a REPORT, where a gap is cosmetic; wrong for a gate input. The RULES are reused and now SHARED rather than copied: `loginFromEmail` and `isBot` from `cli/riskmap.mjs`, and two moves of their own — `legacyLogins` to `cli/epic-state.mjs` (gate.mjs re-exports it, rule 3; importing gate.mjs from the reader would have made a cycle), and `ledgerPersonLogin`, now one function for `yad usage` and the count, because two readers that disagreed would split one person in two or — worse for a number that must not shrink — fold two into one. **The decisions.** (1) **"Active" = committed OR approved**, Part 3's two actions and no more: the Product ledger's approvals and ship engineer-reviews (a platform login since E62), plus git authorship in the Product and every connected repo (a NAME, plus a login only from a `noreply` address). A COMMENT does not count, and neither does whoever MERGED a review PR — `closingRecord` records them and they MEASURE the window, never fill it. (2) **The capacity window reads the Product's OWN merge records** — closing records carrying a PR, plus ships — and NOT merge commits on a default branch: a squash or rebase merge leaves none, so that window would shrink on exactly the teams who squash, and a shorter window means fewer people. Fewer than 20 merges is the WIDE end (180 days), never a short window: a young project has little history, and reading "little history" as "few people" is the quiet under-count. (3) **Every repo in `repos.json`**, not the epic's declared repos: capacity is a fact about the team, and per-epic repos would make one team count differently at each gate. (4) **Identity is `usage.mjs`'s rule and nothing looser** — a `noreply` login joins, `legacyLogins` joins, otherwise TWO rows and the count says how many are name-only. E64: only exact evidence proves identity, and guessing two rows into one makes the count smaller. `recentAuthorsFor` is deliberately NOT reused: it excludes the change's own authors, while here the author IS capacity and the `− 1` is E72's. (5) **Git is asked for TWICE the widest window and the range applied in JS**, because `--since` stops the walk at the first older commit on a chain, so an out-of-order date hides everyone behind it. In E67 that under-LISTED (safe — every name printed was real); here it under-COUNTS. The pad widens the cliff, it does not remove it. (6) **The 120-day gone/stale window is computed but belongs to `yad doctor`**, never to a gate — no gate reads it, and E7's discipline says a printed number nobody must meet is a liability. (7) **Nothing is written to disk and no file shape moves** (E7's stance kept): the count is derived live, so it can never go stale. **Where it prints, computed ONCE per command** so N steps cannot mean N walks of every repo's history: `gate status` (header, in solo mode too, as the per-step count already is), `gate sync` (once, before the per-artifact lines), the generated review-PR body (`**Active people:**` beside `**Approvals needed:**`, so a gate that asks for more people than the team has is visible BEFORE the review starts) — **and that is the ONE surface where the count becomes a lasting record**, since a PR description is written once at `gate open` and read for as long as the PR lives, while the number can differ an hour later or on another machine (a `gate open` run from a laptop that has not cloned the connected repos would leave "not counted" there for good), so the line DATES itself (`… when this PR was opened`) and points at `yad gate status` for the live answer rather than going quiet, the `--json` bundle as an OBJECT and never the sentence, and `yad open-pr` on the Build half. **NOT `checks/*.sh`:** a code repo's CI has no Product on disk (E65's finding b), and E67 is the argument against a third twin — so `routeCount` is HANDED the number by its caller. `activeSum`/`activeBasis` live beside the rule for the reason `gateRuleSum` does, and always say what the number does NOT do. **What the build found.** (a) **The reader asked git for `--since=30 days ago` while filtering on the INJECTED `today`** — a relative date is measured from the real clock, so the two agreed one day a month, the claim "the count reads no clock" was half false, and every fixture test carried a hidden expiry. Git takes `YYYY-MM-DD`; pinned by a test asking with a `today` a year in the future. (b) **`readShardDir` SKIPS a corrupt ship shard** — right for advisory evidence, wrong for a count — so `corruptShards` was added beside it and an unreadable shard makes the answer unknown. (c) **`gatePredicate` takes `active` and never reads it itself**: the golden fixture is copied into a temp directory inside yadflow's OWN work tree, so a walk from there would fold this repo's committers into a frozen snapshot. It is the one predicate input the golden deliberately does not pass, and the snapshot records `null`. **Golden:** `gates` gains `active: null` on all nine; nothing else moves. **What TWO REVIEW ROUNDS found, all fixed with a test, and every one of them the same class — a partial read becoming a NUMBER.** (i) `corruptShards` called `readdirSync` after an `existsSync` that is true for a FILE too, so a stray file named `build-log` under an epic's `.sdlc/` threw ENOTDIR — and the four gate commands call the count unguarded, so the exception escaped and broke `gate sync`, `gate status` and `gate open`, which had worked before. `activePeople` now also catches anything unforeseen and answers `unknown` rather than throwing. (ii) **The timezone bug, and the sharpest one:** `%cd` with `--date=short` renders the date in the COMMIT'S OWN timezone while every other date here is UTC, so a commit made at 08:00 in Tokyo printed tomorrow's date and the range dropped it — `active: 0` with an empty `unknown`. Git is now asked for `%ct` (epoch seconds), and `activeIn` has NO upper bound at all: a future date comes from clock skew, a rebase or a hand-edited ledger, and dropping one makes the count smaller. (iii) A folded `build-log.json` whose `ships` is not a list read as "no ships", losing every engineer-review approver in it; and two ships missing `story`/`task`/`repo` collide on one `readShips` key, so one silently replaces the other. Both are unknown now. (iv) A record we can read whose DATE we cannot was skipped silently; since these dates are written by LLM-driven skills from a `<YYYY-MM-DD>` template, `2026-9-4` is a realistic input, so it is a gap — and the field is named, never the value. (v) `epicIds` drops a folder whose name is not a valid id (and a symlinked one, since `Dirent.isDirectory()` is false for a symlink), taking its approvals with it; anything holding a `.sdlc/` the enumerator did not name is now reported. (vi) **For MERGES the safety direction is INVERTED** — more records push the 20th-newest closer to today, which NARROWS the window and finds FEWER people — and a future merge date made `span` negative, so clamping landed on the narrow end: twenty bad records took the window from 180 days to 30 and printed "-111 day(s)" as the reason. (vii) `yad open-pr` read the count from its working directory, which on the documented Build path IS the code repo: no `epics/`, no `repos.json` and one git log that works, so it reported that repo's committers as the whole team, confidently. It now counts only when standing in a Product. (viii) `gate ci`'s sweep called `gateSync` once per open review PR, so "computed once per command" was false — the sweep now reads it once and hands it down. (ix) `activeSum` goes to stdout and `activeBasis` to stderr, so a captured stdout kept the number and lost the sentence saying it enforces nothing: the disclaimer now travels with the number. (x) `Date.parse` does NOT reject an impossible day — it rolls `2026-02-30` forward to 2 March — so `dayNumber` compares the round-trip. (xi) The lock fix had an off-by-one of its own: a stale lock reclaimed on the LAST try was deleted and the command still reported "another process is writing" about a lock that no longer existed. **Known limits.** In the UNDER-counting direction, and both stated rather than fixed: git's date cut-off can still hide a person behind a commit older than the padded ask; **The identity question is CLOSED, on evidence the writer already records (the user, 2026-09-21).** It was left open because "a post-E62 record names a login" is not true in general — E62 decision 4 falls back to git `user.name` when there is no platform, no network or no login. The marker that tells them apart was already on disk: `upsertBridge` stamps `source: 'bridge'` on every approval it builds from the platform's own answer, and `mapApprovers` builds those as `name: r.login`. So a bridge record names a LOGIN and nothing else does, which is E64's rule — only exact evidence proves identity — applied to a field rather than to the shape of a string. `ledgerPersonLogin` reads that marker, so `yad usage` gains the same truth, and `personKey` now NAMESPACES the two (`login:ada` vs `name:ada`): a git author literally named `ada` and a different human whose login is `ada` used to collapse into one row, an under-count nobody would have noticed. Every intended join still works, because a join is now only ever login-to-login or name-to-name; what no longer joins is a bare name that merely LOOKS like a login, which lands on two rows and over-counts — the safe direction. `nameOnly` is truthful for the first time. A clone merely behind its remote used to belong here; the registry's `syncedHead` now catches the provable case (a clone that does not even hold what was last packed), and only "fetched a while ago but holds it" remains. In the over-counting direction, which is the safe one: a person with two commit addresses is two rows; a ship's `engineer_review` entry carries no `source` marker of its own, so a Build-half approver is keyed by name even when the platform supplied a login, and joins that person's commits only if the strings match; `--since` reads the COMMITTER date, so a rebase refreshes old work into the window; an approval that requested changes still counts as activity; and a robot whose name does not end in `[bot]` counts as a person, because `isBot` is E67's rule and widening it would exclude more people. **Accepted, not fixed:** the committed hub PR template has no `Active people` line, so a review PR opened by hand differs from one `yad gate open` generates — a line a human cannot compute would be worse than the divergence. **Not here:** the cap itself and the `missing.push` that applies it (E72), the solo↔team suggestion (E74), and a `yad doctor` code for the 120-day window, which needs a name in the user's vocabulary **Shipped (#258).** | M | E62 |
| E72 | Base + risk step, capped by capacity. Show and record every cap. **Left by E66:** the Build count is printed by `checks/` scripts that run from the PR's own checkout, so a PR can edit the script that counts it — this row must answer that before the count holds a merge. **The decisions were the user's (2026-09-21), and one of them was reversed after review.** **(1) An unknown count gives NO cap.** `gateCapFor(rule, active)` returns `null` for anything that is not a whole count (null, undefined, NaN, a negative, a fraction, a string): capping on an unknown would invent a number, which E71 forbids. Product CI is usually this case — it checks out the hub only, so `connectedRepos` reads every connected repo as "not on disk" — and `activeSum` says `no cap can be shown, and only the base holds each gate`. **(2) "Record" is an optional `capped: { needed, to, active }` key on the step's closing record** (E18), beside `waived`, written by `gate sync` when a TEAM gate passes while the cap lowered its ask (and, on a Product with no platform, by the `yad-review-gate` skill, which reads the numbers from `yad gate status`). No shape change — the E10 `waived` precedent. Not in solo mode, where nothing was counted; never on the author step. `gate status` prints it back (`count capped from 3 to 1 (2 active people)`). The ask is reported, so the record says what the gate ASKED, not what held it. **(3) The cap is SHOWN, and only the base holds a gate — until E73.** (E73 later kept to detection; enforcement and `yad gate lower --reason` moved to E108.) **This reversed the first answer ("enforce now; E73 next").** Enforcement was built, and an adversarial review then broke its premise with a probe: a commit is keyed by its git NAME unless its address is a platform `noreply` one, an approval by its platform LOGIN, and E71 never joins the two without exact evidence — so an ordinary two-person team that commits with work addresses and approves on GitHub reads as **four**. The cap lowers nothing at four, and an enforced contract gate asks three approvals of a team with one non-author: E7's deadlock, in the NORMAL case, not an edge. So E73 was to turn the capped count on together with `yad gate lower --reason` (rule 7); that moved to E108. What E73 inherits, each found in review: **(a)** the name/login double count above; **(b)** the GitLab gate-sync bot commits as `yad-gate-sync` with no `[bot]`, so `isBot` counts it as a person (GitHub's is excluded); **(c)** every committer in every connected repo counts, including people with no access to the Product; **(d)** a young team gets the WIDE 180-day window, so people who have left still count; **(e)** the count can grow between `gate open` and the merge; **(f)** the predicate runs AFTER the review PR merges, and nothing checks the count before, so an unmet count is found only on a merged PR that cannot take more approvals; **(g)** where the ledger is local, the verdict depends on which machine runs `gate sync`, and making the count unknown (a missing clone) is an unrecorded way to fall back to the base; **(h)** today the only recorded way out of a jam is `yad mode solo --reason`, which waives every open gate on the Product. **(4) The Build count stays REPORTED, and that is the answer to E66's note** — even after E73. A Build merge is held by the platform's branch protection (Part 9), never by this count, so the CI half running from the PR's own checkout stays harmless: nothing blocks on it. `yad open-pr` shows the capped count; `risk-map-check.sh` and `risk-route.sh` cannot cap — a code repo's CI has no Product — and no longer say "until the capacity cap"; `risk-route.sh` says it cannot cap in its output, `risk-map-check.sh` in its header comment. If the Build count ever holds a merge, E66's note comes back first. **Settled from the code, not asked.** `to = max(base, min(needed, max(1, active − 1)))`: the base is 1 and the floor is 1, so the cap can only trim the RISK STEP and never lowers E62's one approval. **The `− 1` is one seat, not a check:** the author wrote the review branch in the Product, whose history is in the count, so they are among `active`; the engine never learns who the author is, and whether the author's own approval counts is the platform's rule (E62 decision h). **Solo mode** prints the cap and records none. `short` now measures against the ASK — the capped count when `active` is known, the full count when not — so a gate can still pass while `short` is not 0. **Surfaces** (as E72 worded them; E73 later removed every "until E73" from the output): `gate sync` and `gate status` (`— capped to 1: 2 active people, less one seat for the author — base enforced, risk step advisory`; a cap that lowered nothing is not named); `activeSum` (`caps each gate's count at 3 approvers (one seat is left for the author); reported, only the base is enforced until E73`); the review-PR body (`1 (enforced) · full count … , capped to 1 for 2 active people when this PR was opened (the risk step is advisory until E73)`) and its Active-people line (`the cap is 1 (one seat is left for the author), so this gate's count is 1`); the `--json` bundle (`step.cap`, an object); `yad open-pr`; `hub-route.sh`. `gateStatus` and `gate review` take an optional precomputed `headCount`, as `gateSync` already did, so a test can show the cap on a fixture with no git history; the CLI passes none. The arithmetic and its words live once, beside the rule (`capLimit`, `peopleWord`, `capSeat`, `capWho`), so E73 has one place to change. **Closed in the third review round:** a step that passes by its SKIP or INHERITED shortcut records no `capped` (nothing was asked); `gate status` prints a hand-edited `capped` only when all three numbers are whole; at 0 or 1 active people every surface says `(never below 1)` instead of subtracting the author's seat (`capSeat`); a hand-edited `capped` whose `to` is not below `needed` is not printed as a cap. **Golden:** `gates` gains `cap: null` on all nine; pass/fail, `missing` and `short` are unchanged. **Costs, stated:** `risk-map-check.sh`, `risk-route.sh` and the two code-repo PR/MR templates change wired bytes, so every wired code repo reads `outdated` until `yad update` (`hub-route.sh` changed too, but nothing installs it). **Not here:** enforcing the capped count, `yad gate lower --reason` and unmeetable-gate detection (E73, with (a)–(h) above as its cases — E73 then kept to detection, and enforcement moved to E108); counting people in Product CI **Shipped (#259).** | S | E66 + E71 |
| E73 | Unmeetable-gate detection. **Shipped as DETECTION only; the decisions were the user's (2026-09-22).** **(1) The row was split.** It was also to turn on the capped count E72 only reports, beside `yad gate lower --reason`. It does not, for two reasons found in the code. First, only the base is enforced, the base is 1 and the floor is 1 (rule 1), so a lower verb would have nothing to lower. Second, E72's case (a) still holds: an ordinary two-person team (work-email commits, GitHub approvals) counts as four, so enforcing would need the way out on every contract gate, which is a broken rule, not an exit. Enforcement, `yad gate lower --reason` and the count fixes they wait for moved to **E108**. **(2) What ships: `gateReach(rule, cap, { have, nameOnly, approvers, days })`** in `cli/epic-state.mjs`, beside the cap's one copy of arithmetic and words. It returns lines saying why a team gate may not pass. Nothing holds a gate: no `missing` entry, no change to `passed` or `short`, no file written, no new field. **Two kinds of line**, because mixing them made lines untrue in review: `may not be met: …` is about TODAY's rule (the one required approval may have nobody but the author to give it), and `if the risk step were enforced: …` is a what-if (today the gate still passes on the base). The checks: **base** — 0 or 1 active person counted and no approval in the counting window; **one person** — exactly two people counted, one NOT MATCHED to a login (a git name, or an approval record that carries no login: an older hand-written one, or an engineer-review record) and one login, and no approval in the window, so the team may be one person (the solo developer who commits with a work address AND through GitHub's web editor reads as two people, and only this check sees it; with more names beside one login — a new team — it does not speak, because that reading is not fair); **full** (what-if) — the cap lowered the ask, and the approvals do not show more people than were counted; **names** (what-if) — the smallest possible team could not give the ask. That team is the larger of the logins and the names (a name may be a second row for one login, so at most as many names as there are logins can overlap), and never fewer than the approvals prove; taking the logins alone made a new four-person team with one web-editor commit read as "as small as 1". A what-if line is not printed when a today line is, and ends "Nothing beyond the one enforced approval is needed today". **"No approval" names the window** (`days`, the capacity window's length): the reader never sees an older approval, and a real run showed "no approval recorded yet" was untrue for a reviewer whose only approval was 200 days old. **Every line talks about the people counted and says "may" or "if"**, because a probe showed the count is wrong in both directions: HIGH for work-email commits (case a), LOW on a new Product, where a reviewer who has never committed or approved is not counted until their first approval. **An approval is evidence** (found in review: without it, the most common GitHub pair — the author commits with a work address, the reviewer only approves — got a today line on every gate). Any approval in the counting window (`approverCount`, from the reader's own list) shows that approvals can be given, so no today line prints. It also sets the smallest team a what-if line assumes: this step's approvals plus the author (`have + 1`), and two once anyone approved anything. That assumes authors cannot approve their own work — always on GitHub, on GitLab only when its settings say so, never on a local ledger (E62 decision h); where it is wrong, the smallest team is guessed too high, so a what-if line may stay quiet when it should speak — never a false alarm from this. A Product whose records are all names (no platform) gets no name line: there is no login for a name to be the same person as. An unknown count (`cap: null`) or `nameOnly` says nothing. **(3) Surfaces: `yad gate status` and `yad gate sync` only** (the user's choice), each under a team review gate that has not passed, prefixed `! `. Both already read the count once per command, so no extra git walk. Not in solo mode, not on a waived (inherited, skipped, deferred) or passed step, not on a done step a sync re-reads. **A line about the whole Product prints once per command** (`uniqueReach`), under the first step it applies to: a real run printed the base line under every review step not reached yet. Not the review-PR body (written once at `gate open`, case e), not the `--json` bundle, not `yad doctor` (it does not count people, and would walk every repo on each run). **Case (f) is only half answered:** a person running `gate status` while the review is open now sees the line before the merge, but wired Product CI never does — it runs only for merged review PRs, and usually cannot count people. **(4) Case (b) fixed here:** yadflow's own GitLab gate bot commits as `yad-gate-sync` <`yad-gate-sync@noreply.<host>`> with no `[bot]`, so it counted as a person and a solo GitLab developer read as two. `isGateBot` in `cli/people.mjs` matches that exact name AND address; `isBot` is not changed, because it has an awk twin in `risk-map-check.sh` held equal by a parity test. This lowers the reported count by one on GitLab Products, which is the true direction: a robot cannot approve. **(5) Wording names no row** (the user's choice): about 60 lines of output, docs, skills, both sites and the templates that said "until E73" (85 counting code comments and tests), now say what is true without a row number (`reported, only the base is enforced`; `(the risk step is advisory)`), so they need no change when the row number moves. Code comments and test names point at E108. **Tests.** Two probes go through the REAL reader on real git history: the ordinary two-person team (four rows, two name-only) and the solo developer who commits two ways (two rows, one name-only). Every guard was mutation-checked — removed once, and a test failed; guards no test could catch were dead code and were removed. A third probe covers the common GitHub pair above. **Pinned tests changed:** the three `E73 FLIPS THIS` tests are now `E108 FLIPS THIS`. Their checks are the same, because a known count is still reported and not enforced; what changed is the name, one output pattern in the over-count test (the `activeSum` line lost `until E73`) and two assertion messages (`until E73` became `until E108`); one other test's message changed the same way (`before E73` became `before E108`). Nine output-string assertions lost `until E73` (eight in `cli/test.mjs`, one in `cli/test-checks.mjs`). **Costs, stated:** `config.yaml` (a comment) changes wired bytes, so every Product reads it `outdated` until `yad update`. The two hub PR/MR templates changed too, but they are copied by hand and not managed, so a Product set up earlier keeps the old "until E73" words until someone copies them again; `hub-route.sh` changed, and nothing installs it. No shape change; the golden is unchanged. **Known limits, handed to E108:** two spellings of one NAME (`bo` and `Bo Chen`) count as two people and no line sees it; an engineer-review approver is keyed by name even when the entry holds a login, because a ship record carries no marker that proves it (E71's stated limit), so the lines call that person "not matched to a platform login"; a line about the full count reads the cap's `active − 1`, so an over-count makes it name more room than there is; and E67's reviewer suggestion (`riskmap.mjs`) can still name the GitLab gate bot, because that reader has a shell twin held equal by a parity test. **Cases (a)–(h) against this row:** (a), (c), (d) are E108's; (b) is fixed here; (e) is not re-checked at the merge; (f) is half answered, above; (g) is unchanged; (h) `yad mode solo --reason` is still the only recorded way out, and the today lines name it | S | E72 |

Cases (c), (d), the UNKNOWN-count question and where `yad gate lower` is recorded are still open, so the row stays parked. **Waits for the count to be accurate enough to hold a merge**, which is E72's cases: **(a)** the git-name / platform-login double count (E71 never joins them without exact evidence — the join needs its own design), and two spellings of one name (`bo` and `Bo Chen`), which E73 cannot see; **(b)** was fixed in E73 (`isGateBot`); **(c)** connected-repo committers who cannot approve Product PRs; **(d)** the wide 180-day window counting people who have left. Nothing is written. **(2) The TEAM direction only.** The count is wrong both ways for ordinary teams (E72 case a, E73), and a wrong "go solo" that a team follows removes every approval quietly — the dangerous direction Part 3 names. The solo direction stays with E73's `! may not be met:` line, which already names `yad mode solo --reason` under a team gate; E74 speaks only in solo mode, where E73 prints nothing, so the two never meet. **(3) "When the count changes" = the count disagrees with the mode that is set.** Nothing is stored (E71's stance): no last-seen count, no new field, no file shape, and no writer to answer for on a verified Product. `profile.team_size` is left alone (typed at setup, read only by setup). The line repeats until someone switches or the evidence leaves the window. **(4) The evidence** (`teamHint` in `cli/people.mjs`, pure): E73's **smallest possible team** — the larger of the platform logins and the names not matched to a login — is 2 or more, OR someone WITH A PLATFORM LOGIN approved in the capacity window, with more than one person counted. The user first chose logins-or-approval; the simulation then showed that a team whose commits all use work addresses, in solo mode and so giving no approvals, reads as names only and was never told, and the user added names. One login and one name (the solo developer who commits with a work address and through GitHub's web editor) is a smallest team of 1 and stays silent. An approval with no login (hand-written on a local ledger, or an engineer-review record) proves nothing and counts only as a name: an author can approve their own work there (E62 decision h), and a noreply commit address makes their commits a login and their approval a name that never join (found in the full review). `active >= 2` stays, because a roster alias can join an older approval to the author's own login. The smallest-team rule is ONE function, `smallestTeam` in `cli/epic-state.mjs`, read by E73's `gateReach` and by `teamHint`. An unknown count (`active: null`) suggests nothing: `yad mode` says so (`the people could not be counted (<why>), so no switch to team mode is suggested`), `yad doctor` warns it with a fix hint (a ✓ beside "could not be counted" read as healthy), and `gate status` (which says `NOT COUNTED`) and `yad next` (the most-run command) do not repeat it — the user's choice after review. **(5) Surfaces — all four, the user's choice:** `yad mode` with no argument (and `--json` as `suggest: { known, line }`, `null` in team mode), `yad gate status` (under the `active people:` line, which already reads the count; an unknown is not repeated there, since `NOT COUNTED` says it), `yad next` (the project view, printed first) and `yad doctor` (`mode:suggest-team`, a **warn**, unknown included). `yad mode`, `yad next` and `yad doctor` read the count **only in solo mode**, once per command (`gate status` already read it in both modes), so team mode pays nothing new — but in solo mode those three now walk the git history of every connected repo on each run, the cost E73 declined for doctor; the user accepted it. One wiring, `soloTeamHint`, and one printer, `printTeamHint`, both in `cli/people.mjs`. Each takes an optional `headCount`, as `gateStatus` does, so a test can pass a count; the CLI passes none.
