After the full review, two more shapes stay silent: a local self-approval under the same name beside noreply commits, and a mistyped future-dated approval by a second login. **Known limits — each reads as more than one person, and the line says "may":** one person with two platform accounts; two spellings of one git name (`ada`, `Ada Lovelace` — E108's join); a robot committing under a plain name with no `[bot]` (`semantic-release-bot`, a hand-set `github-actions`), which E71 already counts as a person; a connected-repo committer who cannot approve Product reviews (E72 case c); someone who left but is inside the window, up to 180 days on a young Product (case d); a workflow that approves PRs automatically (GitHub reports a bot's login without `[bot]`; recording `bot` on an approval belongs with E108's identity work — the user's choice); a GitLab author approving their own MR where the project allows it (dropping GitLab approvals instead would hide real GitLab pairs, the quiet direction); on a local ledger, one person's own approval under a different name from their commits (a second name). | E68 | Suggest reviewers from history; read CODEOWNERS as a hint only. **E67 answers the narrow half** — who committed in the `high` directories a change touches, in 30 days, as an ask the engineer review reports. This row is the general one: any touched folder, a SUGGESTION rather than an ask, and CODEOWNERS read as a hint. **It holds nothing, waives nothing and requests no one: a name it prints is a hint about who MAY know the code, never a claim about who owns it or must approve.** **The row was short, so eight decisions were the user's (2026-09-22):** (1) **`yad open-pr` only**, printed once the PR is open — JS only, so no awk twin and no parity test; the CI check and `risk-route.sh` keep only E67's ask; (2) **the folder each changed file sits in** (`./` for a root file), and only the files DIRECTLY in it, never its subfolders, over **E67's 30 days** — so the output stays one sentence; (3) **one list, ranked by commits in the window then newest, five shown, then `and N more`**; (4) **CODEOWNERS on its own line, never merged with history** and never used to fill an empty one; a team is printed as a team; a line that cannot be read is printed as `not read`; only the documented forms are matched; (5) **never request a reviewer** — E62's decision stands, pinned by a test that the platform call carries no reviewers; (6) **join a git name to a CODEOWNERS login only on exact evidence** — a `noreply` commit address carrying that exact login (printed `also in the 30-day history`); otherwise the output says the same person can appear twice; (7) **anything unreadable says `not read` with why**, never "nobody"; no file says `none`; (8) **GitHub and GitLab, sections included**. **What the build settled.** **One history reader, two users:** E67's git code moved into `historyReader` (the shallow-clone refusal, the change's own authors, and the one `git log` with `--no-merges --no-renames --full-history --since`), and both `recentAuthorsFor` and the new `suggestedAuthorsFor` call it; `rankAuthors` sends each record through `recentAuthors`, so the robot and own-author rules are the same code, never a copy. **One log over every touched folder, not one per folder:** E67 needed one per directory only because its excludes cannot share a query; a folder's own files need no exclude, and one log counts a commit that touched two folders once and costs one git call however many folders there are (capped at 200 for the command line; a cut is printed). **Each folder is a `:(glob)` pathspec with every glob character escaped** (`:(literal)` cannot stop at one level): the magic ends at `)`, so `:weird/` stays a name — the E67 lesson, kept. **The changed files are read without the risk map** (`changedSince`): most repos have none, and `baseChangeLevel` returns the file list only when a map exists. **CODEOWNERS rules were taken from each platform's own docs (2026-09-22), and they differ:** GitHub reads `.github/`, the root, `docs/` in that order, uses gitignore patterns without `!`, `[ ]` or `\`, skips an invalid line, lets `#` start a comment after a pattern, and does not load a file of 3 MB or more; GitLab reads the root, `docs/`, `.gitlab/`, matches a path with no leading `/` at ANY depth (`internal/README.md` matches `docs/internal/README.md`, where GitHub anchors it), has sections with default owners, `^[optional]` sections (a duplicate that is required wins), `!` exclusions that a later line cannot undo, `\ ` for a space, ignores a malformed owner and keeps the rest, and parses an owner written inside a comment. Both are read from the BASE branch, like the risk map, so a change cannot rewrite its own hint. A form its platform's docs do not describe (`[`, `?` or a trailing `/**` on GitLab, any `\` on GitHub) is not matched and the line is printed as `not read`: a wrong match would name the wrong person. **One reading is ours, from both platforms' examples:** a pattern whose last part has no wildcard also covers everything under a directory of that name (`apps/`, `**/logs`), one ending in a wildcard matches files only (`docs/*` never reaches `docs/build-app/`). **No e-mail address is ever printed** (E67's rule): an address owner prints as `an e-mail address`, several as `N e-mail addresses` (counted apart by a key that never leaves the module), and a word on a GitHub line that looks like a mistyped address is printed as `an address-like word`. **The simulation before the first review (20 shapes, real git history)** found wording only — an excluded file read as "matches no line", "1 more lines" — and one shape of its own that was wrong (a `--depth 2` clone of a two-commit repo is not shallow). **Tests found one real bug:** every address prints the same words, so three addresses counted as one. **Every new guard was mutation-checked (34 mutations, none survived);** the git-failure guards are reached by deleting an object from `.git`, and the BOM / CR guards only matter on GitLab (the GitHub path's `trim()` already strips both), so their test is a GitLab one. **What the first review round found, each fixed with a test and mutation-checked (10 guards, none survived):** a committer whose git NAME is an address was printed (now the login stands in, else `a name that is an e-mail address`); a CODEOWNERS word like `@alice@corp.com` was printed in the `not read` warning (any `@` after the first character now makes it address-like); a GitHub `noreply` login was joined to a GitLab `@name` spelled the same, which is a different account (now a login joins only on its own platform — `loginHost`); `GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS=1` in the caller's environment made every `:(glob)` pathspec match nothing while git exited 0, a quiet "nobody" (now the four pathspec variables are removed before every git call in the reader and the CODEOWNERS read — which also fixes E67's JS reader; its bash twin still inherits them); a CODEOWNERS line this reader skipped could have been the last match, so the list now says `so this list may be wrong` whenever a line was skipped; and `also in the history above` could point at someone hidden in `and N more`, so it now says `also in the 30-day history`. The docs said every unreadable line gets a reason (only the first three do) and "every change" (only a code-repo one; `yad ship` prints it too, since it runs `yad open-pr`). **What the second review round found, each fixed with a test and mutation-checked:** a CODEOWNERS line the reader skipped could have been the ONLY match, and the empty answer still said "lists nobody" with no hedge (now `lists nobody this reader could match … so this may be wrong`); `log.showSignature=true`, common where commits are signed, printed git's signature checks into the log and each read as a person — `Good "git" signature … — 3 commits` (now `--no-show-signature`, in both twins); `log.follow=true` makes git crash on a single `:(glob)dir/*` pathspec, so a one-folder suggestion read as "not read" (now `--no-follow` in the JS reader; E67's `:(literal)` pathspecs are unaffected, so the bash twin needs none and has none); and a noreply login is a github.com or gitlab.com account, so it now joins only when the repo's remote is on that public host — a self-managed GitLab or GitHub Enterprise keeps its own accounts. **E67's address leak, fixed with the row (the user's decision, 2026-09-22):** E67's ask printed a git name that is an address as written, in the JS and in the bash twin; the rule now lives in `shownName` (cli/riskmap.mjs) and `shownname` (awk), with the same character class, and the parity test compares them; the bash twin also drops the four pathspec variables. Two guards were deleted because no test could reach them: `--no-show-signature` on the log of the change's own authors (a signature line there only adds an address that matches nobody), and `--no-follow` in the bash twin. **Known limits:** E67's apply (git's date-limited walk stops at the first commit older than the window; two addresses are two rows; `--since` reads the committer date); a CODEOWNERS owner is printed as written, with no check that the account exists; on GitLab an `@name` can be a person or a group, and the output says so; a Shape review PR (`yad gate open`) gets no suggestion, because it reads no code change; GitLab's docs describe no size limit, so none is applied there. **What the third review round found:** the `yad-engineer-review` skill matched an approver only against `(@login)`, so a person now printed as a bare `@carol` read as "could not confirm" instead of "met" (the skill now counts a bare `@login` as a login); and a GitLab heading whose default owners the reader could not split set them to none without a word, so "nobody" came out unhedged (now that line is reported `not read`). **What the fourth review round found:** a GitLab entry whose owner words were all unreadable (`docs/ bob`, `docs/ # note`) took the section's default owners, printing a person where GitLab lists none — the defaults now apply only to an entry that writes no owner, and such a line is reported so the answer is hedged; and the third round's skill change let a git NAME written like a login (`@dave`) read as a real login to the engineer review — so ANY name holding an `@` is now replaced (the login, else `a name that is an e-mail address` or `a name written like a login`), in both twins, and a printed `@word` is always a real login. Two reference files (`check-gates.md`, `risk-routing.md`) described only the old form. **Cost:** `checks/risk-map-check.sh` and `checks/risk-route.sh` change wired bytes (the E67 fix), so every wired repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`; no file shape moves and nothing is stored **Shipped (#262).** | S | E62 + E67 |
| E69 | Warn when CODEOWNERS is stale; `yad codeowners --write` **(dropped — see decision 5)**. **CODEOWNERS stays a hint (Part 3): this row only warns. It never says who owns code, never fails a build, and never writes the file.** **The row was a bare title, so six decisions were the user's (2026-09-22):** (1) **"stale" means a fact yadflow can prove**: a line that matches no file (a GitLab `!` line that excludes nothing too); a second CODEOWNERS the platform never reads (it takes the first of its locations); a GitHub file of 3 MB or more (GitHub does not load it); a line this reader cannot read (E68's `not read`). (2) **Plus one hedged hint**: the `@logins` with no commit in the last **90 days** carrying their `noreply` address — only a person's login (a team, group, role or address is counted as `not checked`), only on a `github.com` or `gitlab.com` remote, and the sentence says they may commit under another address, so it does not mean they have left; on GitLab an `@name` can also be a group, and it says so. 90 days, not E67's 30, so a month of leave is not named. (3) **Where:** a new `yad codeowners check [repo] [--json] [--platform]` plus one `yad doctor` line per connected repo (section `codeowners`, beside `risk-map`). JS only — no bash twin, no parity test; not in `yad open-pr`. (4) **The hint prints in the command only, never in the doctor**: the doctor warns on facts. (5) **`--write` is dropped.** GitHub and GitLab can ENFORCE the file (GitHub's "Require review from Code Owners" branch rule, GitLab's code owner approval on a protected branch), so any name yadflow wrote — even inside a comment, since GitLab parses owners written in a comment — would become an owner the platform enforces: a hint turned into an authority, against Part 3 and against Part 9's "Read CODEOWNERS, do not duplicate". `yad codeowners write` / `--write` is refused with that reason; the team edits the file and commits it through a PR, like the risk map (E65). (6) E69 before E70. **Defaults the user accepted:** every connected repo, or one by name or path (`targets()`, shared with `yad risk-map`); the file and the file list are read **from disk** like `yad risk-map check` (uncommitted files count); no CODEOWNERS is a note, not a warning (a repo with no review rules at all is E70's); anything unreadable says `not known` with why. **What the build settled.** The rules for reading a line are E68's, unchanged; each rule now keeps its `pattern` as written, and `matches` is shared. **A submodule is never called empty:** its files are not in the repo's list, so a line that could reach into one (`vendor/lib/`) is kept, while a line for a file inside it (`vendor/lib/*.js`) is dead — the platform only ever sees the submodule's own path. **The hint uses E67/E68's one history reader** (`historyReader`, now given a word for what a shallow clone lacks) over the whole checked-out branch, with no pathspec: the shallow-clone refusal, the robot rule, the pathspec variables and `--no-show-signature` / `--no-follow` are the same code. A login joins only on its own platform (`loginHost`) and without case. **A pattern holding an `@` is never printed** (the line number stands alone), and no e-mail address is printed anywhere. A first location that is a symlink or a folder is `not known`, never skipped: the platform reads the blob git stores. **The file list is read only when there is a file to check** (it is slow on a large repo, as E65 found), and a list git cannot produce is `not known` — in the doctor too, never a quiet skip; a bare repo has no files on disk and is left to the repos check. **The simulation before the first review (20 shapes, real git history)** found wording only: the shared shallow-clone sentence said "those directories" for a whole branch, and the doctor line named the file that is read as the one out of date when the problem was a second, ignored file. **Every new guard was mutation-checked (38 mutations, none survived)**; four needed a test they lacked (GitLab has no size limit; only a submodule is probed as a folder; the GitLab `excludes` wording; the GitLab group hedge). **What the first review round found, each fixed with a test and mutation-checked (49 mutations over the row, none survived):** a `not read` reason quoted the pattern as written, so `alice@corp.com docs/` printed an address — in E68's `yad open-pr` output too; every quoted pattern now goes through one helper that hides a word with an `@` after its first character; the dead-line check tested every rule against every file and each folder above it — 300 000 files × 1 000 dead lines took 70 s — so each folder is now asked once, a plain path is a set lookup and an any-depth pattern tests the distinct last parts (0.2 s), and a test pins the fast answer to `matches` for every pattern shape; on a disk that ignores case (macOS), `.github/codeowners` was read as `.github/CODEOWNERS`, though git stores the name as written — the name must now match exactly; a path to a folder inside a repo read as `none` — it is now `not known`, since the platform reads the top folder; `yad codeowners check backend --write` ran the check — `--write` is now refused wherever it is typed; a bare repo is no longer called "not a git repo". The docs said the doctor prints "the same facts" (it sums them up, and skips a repo the repos check already reports), showed a pattern that is hidden when it holds an `@`, named only GitHub's enforcement setting, and left out `--platform` from the help. **What the second review round found, each fixed with a test and mutation-checked:** the fast dead-line check read a carriage return in a pattern as the letter `r` (a regex's source writes it `\r`), so a line could be called dead when it was live — only `\` + a character that is not a letter or digit is plain text now; several common shapes still scanned every file (GitLab `gone/api/`, `gone/*.js`, `gone/**/x` — about 23 s for 1 000 dead lines), so a plain part of a pattern that is no name's part now settles the line at once, an any-depth pattern tests the distinct last k parts, and an anchored one only the names that start with its plain head: 0.2–0.5 s for 1 000 dead lines of each measured shape on 300 000 files whose names repeat, and a fixed-seed random test holds the fast answer to `matches`; `--write=true` ran the check — it is refused like `--write`. **What the third review round found (fuzzing, about 6 million cases):** the only disagreement was the reference itself — `patternOf` built its regex without the `s` flag, so `.` stopped at a line break in a folder name (git can list one), and "at any depth" did not reach below it, in E68's `ownersFor` too; the flag is added, with a test. It also measured the cost when every file name is different: shapes that still scan every name (`**/x/**`, `d*/**/f.js`, `*.ext` over 300 000 distinct names) take roughly 8–30 s per 1 000 dead lines — kept, since a real file has tens of dead lines, and recorded below. **The fourth review round was clean** (953 000 fuzzed rules, no disagreement). **A fifth, whole-branch review found** that a repo path typed in another case (`../MyRepo` for `myrepo`) read as "not its top folder" on a disk that ignores case — the check now compares `realpathSync.native` on both sides — and that a refused or unknown action printed plain text under `--json`; it prints `{ ok: false, error }` now. **Known limits:** on a very large repo, hundreds of dead wildcard lines can take seconds, in `yad doctor` too; the hint reads the AUTHOR address, so a person whose `noreply` address is only the committer is not counted; merge commits are left out (`--no-merges`), so a person whose only recent commits are merges is named; E67's apply — git's date-limited walk stops at the first commit older than the window, so out-of-order dates can hide recent work, and `--since` reads the committer date; "matches no file" uses E68's pattern reading, including its one reading of our own. **Cost:** no file shape moves, nothing is stored, and no wired file changes; the `yad-open-pr` skill gains two lines, so its installed copy reads `outdated` until `yad update` **Shipped (#263).** | S | E68 |
| E70 | Warn loudly when a repo has no approval rules at all. **The row was a bare title, so the decisions were the user's (2026-09-22).** **The finding that set the shape: a failed call is not an answer.** GitHub answers 404 on classic branch protection to anyone who is not a repo admin (it needs "Administration: read"), even for a branch its own `branches/<b>` call reports as `protected: true` — checked on cli/cli's `trunk`, where the rules endpoint ("Metadata: read", anyone who can see the repo) listed no review rule. So from an ordinary login "does this branch need approvals?" is often unanswerable, and "no rules" is proven only from calls that SUCCEEDED; anything else is `null`, printed as **not known** with the reason (no CLI, not logged in for that host, offline, 401/403/404, an unreadable answer, a full page of 100, no platform, `YAD_PLATFORM_READ=0`). (1) **An approval rule is a required approval count above 0**: a GitHub ruleset `pull_request` rule (`rules/branches/<b>`, active rules only), GitHub classic `required_pull_request_reviews` (`branches/<b>/protection`), or a GitLab project approval rule that applies to the branch (`approval_rules` — Premium and Ultimate only; on Free an approval never blocks a merge). Protection alone and a code-owner review are printed as facts beside it. GitHub applies the strictest layered rule, so the count is exact only when both were read, else `at least`; several GitLab rules also give `at least`. (2) **`yad doctor` only** — a `protection` section, one line for the Product hub and one per connected repo (`protectionChecks`, reader `cli/protection.mjs`). No new command, no bash twin; `--json` carries the answer (`protection`), with any name holding an `@` hidden. (3) **The branch is yad's** (`default_branch` in hub.json / repos.json, the branch the gates merge into); the line names the platform's default when it differs, and reads the platform's default — saying so — when yad's files name none. (4) **Solo mode prints the facts as `ok`, no banner**, except a required approval, which blocks the solo developer's own merge (`warn`). **This replaced the older `solo-branch-protection` probe** in `projectChecks`, which read classic protection only, asked github.com whatever the Product's host, and treated a failed call as fine. (5) **A warning at most, never a failure** (E69's stance): the platform holds a merge, not yad. Part 3's banner is printed word for word only when BOTH halves are proven — no approval rule AND no branch protection; protected-but-no-approval and not-protected-approvals-unknown have their own sentences. Nothing ever says a repo is safe, or that yad holds a merge. (6) **Rule 8 holds**: read-only calls with the user's own `gh`/`glab` login to the repo's own host (`--hostname` on every call), nothing sent anywhere else; up to four calls per repo on GitHub, four on GitLab, 10 s each. `YAD_PLATFORM_READ=0` turns them off; the test suites set it, as they set `YAD_PLATFORM_LOGIN=0`. A 404 for the BRANCH is the same rule applied once more: the repo was read with this login moments before, so the reason is `so this repo does not have it`, not "or your login may not see it". The `empty` hint stopped offering back the permission its own message ruled out, and that message now says `so this repo has no commits on it yet` for the branch GITHUB named, which does not read as a repo that lacks it. **And round 29's GitLab twin was a transplanted argument:** "the project was read moments before" holds on GitHub, where one permission covers the repo and the branch, but not on GitLab, where reading a project and reading its repository are two settings (a Guest, or `repository_access_level`). Older GitLab issues (#7386, #29576) reported 403 there and asked for 404; GitLab made that change, so the hedge is not a precaution but the right answer. Proven two ways on 2026-09-23: GitLab's `require_repository_enabled!` answers `not_found!("Repository")` before the branch is even looked up, and a live read of gitlab.com project 86809638 gives 200 for the project and 404 `404 Repository Not Found` for `repository/branches/main`. What ended each recurrence was never a better sentence but a rule the grid could check mechanically — a hint may not settle a cause its own message left open; a branch the platform named as its own default is never one the repo lacks; a belief from a 404 may remove an offer, never add certainty. | E109 | Name the cause behind a GitLab 404 instead of hedging, by reading the answer's body. **Left by E70, with the evidence that makes it possible (2026-09-23).** E70's GitLab line has to say `GitLab answered 404 for the branch <b> (it does not exist, or your login may not see it)`, because yad cannot tell the two apart: `api()` in `cli/protection.mjs` keeps only the HTTP status and throws the body away, so a missing branch and a repository the login may not read look identical. **GitLab does tell them apart.** Its branches API runs `require_repository_enabled!` before it ever looks the branch up, and that helper answers `not_found!("Repository")` — so the body is `{"message":"404 Repository Not Found"}` for a repository you may not read and `{"message":"404 Branch Not Found"}` for a branch that is not there. Checked live on gitlab.com: project 86809638 answers 200, and its `repository/branches/main` answers 404 `404 Repository Not Found`. **The shape this row must keep.** (1) `api()` keeps the body of a failed call as well as its status, which every other reader in the file then has available. (2) A body may only REMOVE a cause from a sentence, never add certainty — round 30's rule, and the reason this is not just string matching: a message is not an API contract. (3) A body yad does not recognise keeps today's hedge word for word, so a GitLab that reworded the message, or answers in another language on a self-hosted server, loses nothing. (4) The two new sentences say what they proved and no more: the project's repository could not be read (name who can grant it), or this project does not have the branch. (5) The wording grid gains both shapes, and the hint rule already in it holds — a hint may not settle a cause its own message left open. **Why it is worth a row and not a patch:** it changes `api()`, which every call in the reader goes through, and it turns a hedge that is currently CORRECT into two assertions, which is exactly the move that cost E70 eight review rounds. Not here: GitHub, whose 404 bodies do not separate these cases; any other endpoint's body; retrying a failed call. **Shipped (2026-09-23).** Both bodies were checked again with glab 1.77, which prints the body as JSON on stdout: gitlab.com project 86809638's `main` answers `404 Repository Not Found`, and a branch gitlab-org/gitlab does not have answers `404 Branch Not Found`. GitLab's source shows what the first one proves: `require_repository_enabled!` answers it when `feature_available?(:repository, current_user)` is false, which means the repository is turned off for the project OR the login's access is too low to read it. So that sentence names the proven fact in the row's own words (this project's repository could not be read) and keeps both of those causes open. It does not say "your login cannot read it", because when the repository is turned off nobody can. **How it landed.** (1) `api()` keeps a failed call's parsed body (`body`, or null when it is not JSON). (2) `gitlabBranchCause` recognises only the two EXACT messages, and only under a 404. A lower-case, reworded, translated, padded or non-string message is null, and so is `toString`, because the lookup is `Object.hasOwn`. (3) `404 Branch Not Found` prints GitHub's two sentences with "project" in place of "repo" (`…so this project does not have it` / `…has no commits on it yet`, split by `namedByPlatform` as before). `404 Repository Not Found` is a new kind, `no-repository`, that says nothing about the branch (it was never looked up), with a hint to ask a Maintainer or Owner. Any other body keeps E70's hedge and hints word for word. (4) **A new field, `cause` (`'branch'` \| `'repository'`), set only when a 404 PROVED why.** The hints now turn on it, not on the platform's name: the access tail on `no-branch` and `empty` is dropped exactly when `cause === 'branch'`. GitHub's branch 404 sets `cause: 'branch'` too, because one permission covers the repo read and the branch, so the same proof reads the same on both platforms (rule 3 of E70: a hint may not reopen a cause its message ruled out). `cause` is in `--json`. (5) The wording grid gains four shapes (each body, a translated body, and `Repository Not Found` on the PROJECT read, which changes nothing) and one rule with a firing count: a line whose 404 proved the cause may not hedge, a proven-missing branch may not be offered access, and a proven-unreadable repository may not have its branch settled. E70's platform-default rule now exempts `cause: 'repository'`, because there the branch was never looked up. Distinct lines went from 1358 to 1374. **What the review found.** GitLab shows a project's `default_branch` only to a login that may read its code (`expose :default_branch_or_main … if: … :read_code` in `basic_project_details.rb`; live, project 86809638's answer has no such key). So `404 Repository Not Found` never arrives beside a platform default, and the E109 tests now use that real shape, keeping one labelled synthetic case as a defence. The same fact leaves a twin this row does not reach, recorded as E110. Also fixed: a stale comment above `namedByPlatform`, the CLI.md row that left out half the hint, two E70 pins that built a hint shape no reader produces, and the `no-repository` hint, which now names one action per open cause (an Owner turns the repository on; otherwise a Maintainer or Owner grants access) | S | E70 |
| E110 | On GitLab, a project answer with no `default_branch` keeps the access cause open. **Left by E109's review, with the evidence (2026-09-23).** GitLab exposes `default_branch` only when the login has `read_code` on the project (`lib/api/entities/basic_project_details.rb`), and checked live: gitlab.com project 86809638 answers 200 with NO `default_branch` key, while its branch read answers `404 Repository Not Found`. GitHub is unchanged word for word: its default is shown to anyone who may read the repo, which the answer just proved. A branch yad's files DO name is asked for directly, so a missing default changes nothing there. **(3) The grid** gains GitLab project answers with `default_branch: null` and `""` beside the real missing key, and a rule keyed on the field, with a firing count (6): a missing GitLab default names both causes and an action for each, and a missing default with no second cause offers no access. Checked by mutation. Distinct lines 1374 → 1380. **What the review found (two reviewers, both approved).** The same twin class again: the E110 hint told a non-owner only to ask for access, which cannot help while the repository is turned off, and E109's `no-repository` hint also offers "or to turn it on". | E19 | `.sdlc/index.json` — the one-file front door. **The row was a bare title, so the decisions were the user's (2026-09-23).** **The finding that set the shape:** the code already moved AWAY from one shared file written by many branches — `cli/ledger.mjs` sharded the Build ledgers because two people on one epic both appended to one file and conflicted at merge, and "Avoiding collisions" (Part 4) says one small file per writer. An index updated on every write would bring that conflict back for every pair of PRs, on different epics. **(1) A committed, DERIVED file, written on the default branch only.** It is rebuilt from `epicIds()` (one enumerator — the Foundation included, no third copy beside `usage.mjs`'s) plus each item's `.sdlc/state.json` and `epic.md` frontmatter, and never edited by hand (phase 5's founding rule: pure cache, rebuildable from git, never the system of record). On a verified Product, `gate ci --merged` rebuilds it right after it writes the ledger, in the same allowlisted commit, and `ledger-guard` protects it (rule 9: the ledger is written by one trusted identity). On a local Product, a gate write rebuilds it only when the current branch is the default branch, and `yad index` rebuilds it on demand. `yad migrate` regenerates it, never migrates it. **(2) A summary per work item, not a mirror of every step:** id, kind, type, theme, parent and thread, profile, current step, created date, repos, a count of steps in each of the eight step states (E38), and the last closing record's date and closer (E18). An item whose files will not parse is LISTED and marked `unreadable` with the reason — never dropped (a silently missing item is the quiet under-count E71 warned about) and never allowed to stop the whole file. `yad history show` (E20) reads the item's own files for detail. **(3) Staleness is a hash of the exact bytes the index was built from**, the `docs-build.json` precedent, not a HEAD sha, which moves on commits that touch no work item. `yad doctor` recomputes it and says the index is behind, with `yad index` as the fix; Approving never advances, the way approving a PR never merges it. **(2) No platform only.** With a platform the approvals are the PR/MR's, so all three verbs refuse and name `yad gate sync`; on a verified ledger they name CI. One check (`hub.platform`) covers both, since a verified ledger needs a platform. **(3) An approval records `artifactHash`**, so an edit revokes it as it revokes a platform approval; an older hand-written record with no fingerprint still counts. **(4) `comment --by <name> [--count <n>] [--new-round]`** records participation in `comments.json`, one record per `(step, commenter, round)`; the words stay in `reviews/*--comments.md`. With no platform a comment never holds the gate. **Found in the build:** `advanceState` stamped every close `via: "merge"`; it now takes `via: "approved"` from `advance`, with no `pr` or `commit` (E18). **Rules the build set:** a review not opened yet is refused by all three verbs, naming `yad gate open` (recording there would let `advance` close the author step with no record); a step already passed is refused by `advance` (a gate advances once) but not by `approve`/`comment`, which record against today's content, as `gate sync` re-syncs a done step; `advance` refuses a deferred step and points at `yad undefer` (`approve`/`comment` record against it, since its review is still owed). `--by` is refused, not cleaned, if it holds a control or bidi character, a line break, or a space at either end, because it is printed on other people's screens (as E111 treats a title). `approve` warns, and still records, when `--by` is the epic's `owner:` or the artifact's last git author — the engine cannot tell who is typing. A repeat with the same content is byte-identical. **Review round 1 added:** `approve` writes the ledger only — the dated `reviews/*--approved.md` is the skill's full record, which the short `gate sync` list would have erased; a name that differs only by case from one already on the step is refused (the gate counts names exactly, so `Bob` after `bob` would be two approvers); a round is one version of the artifact — each comment record carries `artifactHash`, and `--new-round` opens a round only when the artifact changed since the latest one, so a retry or a second reviewer joins it; a round written as text reads as the number. **It does NOT stop a merge conflict:** the writers upsert (one record per person) and then sort (`canonicalApprovals`/`canonicalComments`), so equal content gives equal bytes; after a clash a person keeps either side and runs the verb again. **No shape change** (the same record keys as before, plus `artifactHash`, which bridge records already carry). It closes one of E17b's two leftovers; `yad-backfill promote` still needs its own verb **Shipped (#274).** | M | E17b |
| E43 | Background capture to `yad/wip/<name>` — shipped as **`yad capture`**. The row was a bare title, so the decisions were the user's (2026-09-25). **(1) Trigger:** the command, run after every agent edit by a harness hook — Claude Code `PostToolUse`, Cursor `afterFileEdit` — and by hand from any other tool. Every capture takes EVERY changed artifact, so a person's own editor edits ride along. Not a file-watcher daemon: a process nobody sees, and one that behaves differently on Windows. **(2) Push:** the commit is local and immediate; the hook pushes in a detached background process at most once every 5 minutes, with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, ssh `BatchMode` and a low-speed timeout, so an edit never waits on the network. A capture by hand pushes at once. No remote or offline: local only, said, never a failure. **(3) Branch:** `yad/wip/<git user.name, lower-cased and made ref-safe>/<epic>` — one branch per person per epic. The git name, not the platform login: it is there offline and never flips, so one person's work never splits across two branches. Limit: two people with one git name share branches. **(4) Files:** everything under `epics/<epic>/` and `foundation/` EXCEPT the ledger — any `.sdlc/` folder and `reviews/` — with the two person-written `.sdlc/` files, `contract-lock.json` and `change.json`, included. The complement of the ledger, not a fourth artifact list. **The mechanism never touches the checkout:** git plumbing in a throwaway index (`read-tree HEAD` → `add -A` of the epic's changed paths → `write-tree` → `commit-tree --no-gpg-sign` → `update-ref <new> <old>`, a compare-and-swap retried once). The person's branch, index and files are exactly as they were, and no git hook runs. A capture with nothing new (the artifacts equal the branch tip) commits nothing. A branch whose edits were undone is captured back to what is on disk, but only while HEAD is still the one it was last captured from — once HEAD moves, a difference is other work arriving, and recording it would add a commit to every old branch on every pull. **The commit shape is a contract E44 reads:** `wip(<epic>): capture` with `Yad-Epic`, `Yad-Base` (the HEAD the tree was built on, or `none`) and `Yad-Branch` trailers. The commits are unsigned on purpose — a signing prompt inside a hook would hang the agent; E44's fold is the commit that is signed. **Wiring:** `hooks/yad-capture.sh` plus a `CAPTURE_ADAPTERS` entry per harness, through the guard's merge/unmerge/doctor machinery — but in BOTH ledger modes, on any Product whose config does not say `"capture": false` (which unwires it; `YAD_CAPTURE=0` is one shell's choice and never unwires a committed file). The hook always exits 0 and prints nothing to stdout; `yad capture --hook` has its own fast path in `bin/yad.mjs`. **CI, and a finding:** the Part 4 sentence "`yad setup` writes the exclusion" cannot be literally true — a local-ledger Product has no yad CI at all, and a team's own workflows are theirs. So `yad-update-guard.yml`, the one yad template a push to any branch starts, now says `branches-ignore: ["yad/wip/**"]` (new bytes: every wired repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`), and `yad doctor` names each of the team's own workflows a push to `yad/wip/*` would start. **The people count (E71) does not count capture commits:** `git log --all` now excludes `refs/heads/yad/wip/*` and `refs/remotes/*/yad/wip/*` — a draft snapshot is nobody committing work. **Review round 1 found and fixed:** a Product in a subfolder of its repository captured nothing (git prints paths from the repo top; they are now cut to the Product with `--show-prefix`); a file staged and then deleted made `git add` refuse the whole epic on every capture (a path neither on disk nor in HEAD is dropped); `--force-with-lease` refused every push after the first in a single-branch clone and could overwrite a second machine after an IDE's background fetch — the push is now plain, a fast-forward that is refused rather than forced; a git name with no Latin letters gave no branch (it falls back to the email's local part, then a fingerprint); `git status` briefly locked the real index (`GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`); the throttle is per clone (`--git-common-dir`), not per worktree; the hook script no longer waits on a terminal's stdin; `yad doctor`'s workflow scan reads only the top-level `on:` block and judges a filter by whether it keeps `yad/wip/*` out; a `.claude` folder whose only yad hook is capture is still detected as a target. **Review round 2 found and fixed:** with no local capture branch but one on origin (a fresh clone, a deleted branch), captures restarted from HEAD and every plain push was refused in silence — a capture now continues origin's branch, a capture by hand fetches this person's capture branches first, and a refused push is named as stuck (it stays refused while two machines each keep their own copy; the fix is `git branch -D` on one), never "rides the next push"; a blank line inside a `branches:` list no longer hid it from `yad doctor`. **Review round 3 found and fixed:** the fetch now prunes this person's capture copies, so a machine with no local branch does not continue one deleted on origin — but a machine that still holds the local branch, or a saved copy of origin's (a plain `git fetch` or an IDE's background fetch leaves one), rebuilds it and pushes it back, by hand or from the hook — so removing a capture branch for good means deleting it on origin first, then `git branch -D` and `git branch -dr origin/…` on every machine that has it — not `git fetch --prune`, which a single-branch clone does not apply to these refs (reviews 4–6; stated in the README); the fetch has the push's low-speed limit; `yad doctor` reads `[a-z]` in a branch pattern as GitHub does; the refused-push hint names the branch. **No shape change.** **Not here:** the fold (E44), file claims (E46), Windows (E113) **Shipped (#275).** | M | — |
| E114 | Rename-blind changed lists in `contract-check` and `backfill-check` — **fixed** (2026-09-26). **Left by E47's review 4, older than E47.** Both scripts built their changed list with plain `git diff --name-only`, which names a rename by its NEW path only: `git mv specs/S1/contracts/api.md docs/api.md` passed contract-check with no `Contract-Change` trailer, and `git mv src/<feature>/x.js lib/x.js` passed backfill-check while that feature's spec was not approved. **Now every such line is `git diff --no-renames --name-only -z … \| tr '\0' '\n'`:** a rename is listed by both paths, and `-z` means git never quotes a path (it quotes one holding a `"` or a tab even with `core.quotePath=false` — E47's review 4 finding, here too). **Both of contract-check's lists:** the `deleted` list behind the lockless escape hatch (a `--diff-filter=D` line) gets the same flags, or a slice MOVED out of `contracts/` would show as an `R`, miss the filter, and the hatch would refuse the one fix a short lane has. **Bytes, not characters:** both scripts `export LC_ALL=C`, so a file name that is not valid UTF-8 cannot make GNU grep (Linux CI) drop the list — E47's review 5 finding. **Tests run each script on a real repo with a real `git mv`** (and a `"`-named and a non-UTF-8-named file, the last written through the index); Its hint names the whole template line, not only `--no-renames`, which alone still lets git quote a path past the gate. `yad check --fix` refreshes a wired contract-check nobody changed; one changed by hand is kept, and backfill-check is never wired (every copy is placed by hand), so the warning is the only thing that names them. | E115 | A symlink can change the contract surface unseen — **fixed** (2026-09-26). **Left by E114's review 1, older than E114.** contract-check reads PATHS, and a link lets the content live where no path under `specs/` names it. Checked on scratch repos, six holes passed: a symlink AT `src/<feature>` (listed as `src/<feature>`, no slash, so no feature), **every later edit to its target**, a link inside `src/<feature>/`, `src` itself as a link, and two the row did not name — **a PR could approve itself** (the spec was read from the PR's working tree, so setting `verified: true` in the same PR passed, and so did deleting the spec: "not being backfilled — skipped"), and a second spelling (`Src/billing/`, `src/Billing/`) that is one folder on a Mac and a different one on Linux CI. **The user's decisions (2026-09-26): (1) refuse links only where a feature being backfilled lives** — a symlink (120000) or submodule (160000) at `src`, at `src/<feature>` or inside it, for a feature with a spec on the base; links elsewhere in `src/` stay legal, since a code repo may hold real ones. Spec folders fold too (`specs/backfill/Billing/spec.md` is `billing`'s spec). **Not a dead end:** a deleted symlink or submodule is not a change to the feature (the changed list is now `git diff --no-renames --raw -z`, which carries each path's modes), so the PR that removes a link passes even while the feature is unapproved — otherwise that PR and the approval PR would each wait on the other. A link on the base where the spec lives (`specs/backfill/<feature>` or its `spec.md`) makes that feature unapproved; one at `specs` or `specs/backfill` fails every feature change until a PR puts real files back (that PR changes no feature). | E117 | `link.md` chooses where the lock is read from — **fixed** (2026-09-26). **Left by E115's review 1, older than it.** The Product-reading gates find the Product through `product-repo` in `specs/<story>/link.md` — a file the PR's own author writes. **Two holes, not one:** a PR could point it at a folder it commits itself, holding a hand-made `contract-lock.json` with the hash it pins ("hash matches"); and, simpler, at a path that does not exist — the gate called the Product "not reachable", deferred, and PASSED. **The user's decisions (2026-09-26): (1) `product-repo` comes from `link.md` as it stands on the BASE** when the base has a value; a PR that changes it gets a note, and the new value counts once it merges. A `link.md` new in the PR, or a base one with no value, takes the value of the first `specs/*/link.md` on the base that has one — where the Product lives is a fact about the repo — and the PR's own value only when no base `link.md` names one (the repo's first spec). Only `product-repo` comes from the base: `contract-lock` is read as the PR leaves it, because a re-spec PR must update the pin. **(2) A Product folder the code repo TRACKS is read as it stands on the base commit** (its `epics/` written out through a throwaway index, `git read-tree` + `git checkout-index`) — but only when the base tracks its `.sdlc/hub.json`, which every Product commits; any other tracked file under a Product path is **FAIL** by name; so is a path through a tracked symlink, submodule or file. The row first said "refuse a path inside the code repo"; that was the wrong test twice over: CI can only check a second repo out INSIDE the workspace (`actions/checkout` `path:`), and a monorepo (`yad setup --monorepo`) keeps the Product and the code in one git repo, so its lock is always tracked — a plain FAIL broke it, and the user chose the base read instead. **Stated cost:** in a monorepo a PR that re-locks the contract AND changes the slice fails the pin check; the re-lock merges first. **(3) All four gates** (contract-check, lineage-check, epic-open, reconcile-debt-check) — the fix lives in the shared block, which stays byte-identical. **(4) `yad doctor` warns `checks:product-path-blind`** for an older copy of any of the four (no `git show`, or no `git checkout-index`). Case is folded only where git folds it (`core.ignorecase`), ASCII only. A Product absent from CI still defers with a note, as before. Tests, per gate: a Product the PR commits; a re-pointed `product-repo` (to nothing, and to an untracked Product that would pass); a monorepo Product in a folder and at the repo root, edited by the PR; a tracked symlink, dangling symlink and submodule on the way; plus a base `link.md` with no value and an untracked Product inside the repo, read as before. 20 of the 22 new tests fail on the old gates. **Review 1 found and fixed (the user's decisions on three):** a story NEW in the PR still used the PR's value, so a made-up `EP-sealed-S99` pointing at nothing deferred in all four gates — now a sibling's base value; a story written as a path (`Task: EP-x-S01/.-T1`) named a `link.md` the base read never found — one story-ID pattern, `EP-<slug>-S<n>` with a lowercase slug, now FAILs by name in the four gates and in spec-link; one file force-added under an ignored Product checkout switched every gate to a partial Product read from the base (a sealed epic read as open) — hence the `hub.json` test; a folder named `-p` was read by `cd` as an option and the walk took it as untracked (`cd -P ./<part>` now); and the first archive cache keyed the repo root as `""`, which an empty key took as done — a deferral, caught by the tests before it shipped. The first extraction also used `git archive`, which drops what the base's `.gitattributes` marks `export-ignore` — a Product so marked came out empty; it is now a throwaway index. E115's `checks:symlink-blind` looked for any `git ls-tree`, which the sibling lookup (of the base) also runs — it now looks for one on `HEAD`. **Review 2 found and fixed (the user's decisions on two):** whether the Product is kept in the repo was decided on the PR's disk, so a PR that deleted or moved a monorepo's Product folder deferred in all four gates — it is now asked of the base, from the path's text alone (`lex_rel`); `git checkout-index` took attributes from the PR's work tree, so a `.gitattributes` of `*.md text eol=crlf` or `working-tree-encoding=UTF-16` wrote the base's files unreadable and every gate passed — the work tree now points at the empty temp folder; `fm_val` matched the `---` fences before stripping CR, so any CRLF file read as empty; a symlink or submodule inside the base's `epics/` was written out and followed (an absolute link let the PR supply the lock) — now refused by name; one stale sibling `link.md` gave every new story a deferral — a sibling of the same epic now comes first, and the first that resolves; a slice added or changed with no `link.md` deferred (E115's stated cost) and a slice for an epic missing from a reached Product said "not reachable" — **the user chose FAIL for both**, with a delete-only story still allowed; a Spec Kit folder (`specs/001-name/contracts/`) is refused when it changes (**the user's call**) and allowed when removed; reconcile-debt called a reached Product without the epic "not reachable" — now an orphan FAIL, as its twins say; `yad commit --task` accepted IDs spec-link now refuses — `TASK_ID_RE` matches it. **Review 3 found and fixed (the user asked for every finding fixed, the safer choice where one was needed):** "only removes" counted a PR that deleted `link.md` with PART of a slice, so the rest stayed pinned by nothing — `link.md` may now go only when the whole slice is gone at HEAD; a monorepo Product whose base `product-repo` was spelled past the repo's own folder name (`../../../repo/product`) or as another machine's absolute path was not recognised, so deleting it deferred again — a Product the base keeps (a tracked `.sdlc/hub.json` with an `epics/` folder beside it) is now THE Product whatever `product-repo` says, which also closes a first spec pointing nowhere; a `.gitattributes` merged into the base's `epics/` re-encoded the copy as UTF-16 — its entries are now dropped from the throwaway index; contract-check took a reached folder with no `epics/` as "not reachable" where its twins said orphan, and refused to remove an orphan's slice — both fixed; `awk -v` read a `\b` in a folder name as a backspace — the prefix now goes in through the environment. **Review 4 found and fixed a regression from review 3:** the kept-Product rule ran before the disk was looked at, so ANY tracked `.sdlc/hub.json` beside an `epics/` folder — a test fixture, a docs example — was read instead of the real Product CI checked out; one merged fixture then let a stale pin or a sealed epic pass, and a repo with two such folders (yadflow itself has two) failed every story. Now a kept Product is read only when `product-repo` reaches nothing — no kept Product by its text, no folder on disk — and only one holding the story's epic (two that hold it FAIL). And an empty `product-repo` with a kept Product was "read" while three gates still skipped the check on the empty value — the chosen Product now sets it. **Stated:** in a two-repo setup whose CI checks nothing out, a fixture that holds the epic is read where the gate used to defer — no weaker than the deferral it replaces. **Review 5 found and fixed:** on Linux, a `product-repo` of `/proc/self/cwd/<folder>` — a magic link that means a different folder to each process — let the part-by-part walk (run in a subshell) see its own folder and nothing tracked, while the gate's own shell read the PR's edited copy of a kept Product through it; the path is now also resolved in ONE step from the gate's shell (`cd -P` + `pwd -P`) and that real location walked again, which catches every such alias.
