"run the command you choose" was untrue for a turned-off plugin or a tool set up by hand (now "follow the steps you choose"). **Review round 2 found, all fixed:** round 1's fix looked in the raw folder you ran yad in, and finding never walks up — so `check` from a subfolder (`epics/sub`, `api/src`) called every tool installed at the repo's top missing; `check`, `update` and `yad toolbox list/check` now all look at the top of the repo you are in (the registered code repo, else the Product); `check --dir <a code repo>` read the choices from the code repo (none) instead of its Product; join's closing lines now sit under a `Next` heading, apart from the Toolbox list; two E86 sentences still promised an install command for every tool. **Review round 3 found, fixed:** round 2's fix swapped a git worktree inside a registered repo (`api/.claude/worktrees/x`, or one beside the Product through the workspace file) for the registered repo's main checkout — the E80 review 3 mistake again — and a lone repo's subfolder was not lifted to its top. One function, `toolsFolder` in `cli/find-product.mjs`, now takes the DEEPEST of the git checkout you are in, the registered repo holding you (`apps/web` in a monorepo is not a checkout of its own) and the Product; it reads only whether `.git` exists and runs nothing. **Review round 4 found, fixed:** a folder reached through a link was not lifted to its checkout's top, so `check` (given the shell's spelling) and `yad toolbox check` (given the one on disk) could disagree — the top is now looked for under both spellings. **Round 5 was clean.** **Not here:** a link inside the Product into another repo: tools are looked for in that repo (the checkout that holds the folder) while the choices are still the Product's, found through the link like every Product command; an MCP server added with Claude Code's local scope while it was started in a subfolder is recorded under that subfolder in `~/.claude.json`, and the toolbox, looking at the top, does not see it (unverified, and rare); a prompt per tool, or a `skip` written from setup (decided against); running an install; skills reading their fallback from the toolbox (E87); version ranges (E88). | M | E84 + E50 |
| E86 | `yad toolbox add / remove / list / check` — shipped as **`.sdlc/toolbox.json`** in the Product and three new actions. **The row was a bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-10-01).** (1) **`add` records only.** It writes the team's choice and prints the install command; nothing installs a tool or starts a program (E85's "offer, never auto-install"). (2) **The file is `.sdlc/toolbox.json` in the Product, committed**, so the team shares one answer; `add` and `remove` are Product writers and refuse with no Product (E80), from inside a registered code repo they write the Product's file. (3) **Shipped and custom tools.** `add <id>` marks a shipped tool as used here; `add <id> --custom --role … --fallback … --detect <kind>:<name>[,…] [--install "<type>: <command>"] [--source <https URL>]` adds a team's own; `remove <id>` drops a custom tool or marks a shipped one as not used. (4) **`check` reports and exits 0**: each tool in use that is missing, turned off, or outside its range, with its install commands and fallback. `toolboxCheck()` is the function E85 calls. **Rules the row implied:** **in use by default** = the core tools plus the connector `design.json`, `testing.json` or `learning.json` names (a connector id the toolbox knows; `none` and other adapters name nothing); the file holds **only differences from that default** — `shipped: { id: "use" \| "skip" }` and `custom: [...]` — so a later release that changes a default is not overruled by a line nobody chose; `remove` does not disconnect (the connect skill owns `design.json`), and says so; **finding still reads the folder yad runs in** (a code repo's own skills count) while the choices come from the Product, so `toolbox` stays out of `PRODUCT_CMDS`; the file is read strictly before a write (a broken file is refused, never rebuilt), stamped with `schemaVersion` by its writer (no shape change: a new optional file), and keys a release does not know are kept; a line that does nothing (an unknown id — a newer release's tool or a typo —, a value other than `use`/`skip`, a custom entry missing a part, a duplicate) is ignored by `list`/`check` and named by `yad doctor` (`toolbox`, warn, new code `YAD-CFG-007`; a broken file fails), and `remove <id>` clears whatever the file holds under that id — doctor never reports a missing tool, as decided; with `--dir`, a writer takes the Product folder itself (as `yad skill bind` does) while `list`/`check` may name a code repo; a custom `bins` entry is a program name, never a path; every word of a custom entry is cleaned before it is printed (the shared file can hold control characters); ids are `^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]*$` before they become a key, so `__proto__` cannot reach the map; writers refuse on settings drift (E122) like every other writer. **Review round 1 found, all fixed:** `add`/`remove` said "nothing to change" while deleting a line that did nothing (whether the file changed and whether the tool's use changed are two questions); `add` answered a custom entry the list ignores as in use; doctor's advice to `remove` and add again failed for an unshipped line and a custom entry with a bad id (`remove` now clears whatever the file holds under an id); with `--dir`, a writer walked up to a Product instead of taking the folder as the Product, as `yad skill bind` does; `list`/`check` did not say why no Product was found; a custom `bins` entry could be a path. **Round 2 found, all fixed:** a custom entry sharing a shipped tool's id made `remove` drop only that entry and answer `used: false` while the tool stayed in use — one `remove` now clears everything under the id in one write; `add` judged a broken copy before a good one as unused, against the list (it now asks `loadChoices`, the list's own reader). **Round 3 found, all fixed:** the "undo with …" hint was printed after clearing a line that did nothing, where the opposite command would switch the tool on or off instead — it is offered only when the tool's use changed; a remove of only a dead custom entry was said twice; `add` now also clears a dead custom entry under a shipped id, as `remove` does; an entry with an empty id is flagged as fix-by-hand. **Round 4 found, fixed:** the twin of round 3 — `add` still said "nothing to change" beside a write that removed a dead copy; both verbs now report a shipped-tool edit through one function (`sayShippedEdit`), so they cannot say it differently again. **Round 5 was clean** (a 144-run CLI matrix); its three notes were done in round 6: `remove` prints the fallback after "Without it:", as `list` does (ECC's "nothing changes: …" read as if nothing happened), a dead copy's reason reads "(the id is a shipped tool's)", and a comment says why removing only dead copies never changes a tool's use. **Not here:** offering or installing at setup and update (E85); skills reading their fallback from the toolbox (E87); version ranges (E88); a custom tool's version range; editing a custom tool in place (remove and add it again); `--custom` with more than one install route from the command line (the file takes a list). | S | E84 |
| E87 | Every skill declares its fallback when its tool is missing — shipped as a **When a tool is missing** section in nine skills, built by **`skillFallbackSection()`** in `cli/toolbox.mjs` and written by **`scripts/skill-fallbacks.mjs`**. **The row was a bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-10-01).** (1) **A body section, never a frontmatter key**: the agent reads the body, and the description lines stay as they are (they are near the 1024-character cap). (2) **The toolbox owns the words**: each section quotes the entry's `fallback` and `records` word for word, and a test holds them in both directions — a skill a tool's `usedBy` names has the section, exactly as built, and a skill with the section is one a tool names; every `usedBy` entry is a `yad` command or a skill folder. (3) **A skill asks yad, and a team's `skip` is followed**: the skill runs `yad toolbox list --json` and uses a core tool when its entry says `used: true` and the state is `installed` or `available`; after `yad toolbox remove spec-kit`, `yad-spec` hand-writes the spec even where Spec Kit is installed. A connector still follows the `design.json`, `testing.json` or `learning.json` its connect skill writes (E86: `remove` does not disconnect). If yad cannot answer, the skill's own check, as before. (4) **Toolbox tools only.** **Rules the row implied:** the skills are `yad-spec`, `yad-ui`, `yad-connect-repos`, `yad-backfill`, `yad-test-cases`, `yad-learn` and the three `yad-connect-*`; recommended tools are bound with `yad skill bind`, so no skill names one; the section sits before the skill's first `##` heading, so it is read early; a fallback never ends in a full stop (the section adds it) and a connector names the `yad-connect-*` skill whose file connects it (`toolProblems`); the record means "not used, whatever the reason", so a skip does not invent a new value. **What the survey found, fixed here:** Repomix had two absence records (`repomix: unavailable` in a backfill spec, `source: repomix-unavailable` in `repos.json`) and two fallback stories ("the code map alone" against "put together by hand"), its `records` was null, and `yad-backfill` was missing from its `usedBy`. Now one record, `source: repomix-unavailable` — the backfill frontmatter already had a `source:` key, and nothing in `cli/` reads either — and one fallback sentence that covers the commands and both skills. **Review round 1 found, all fixed:** the new test failed on the Windows CI leg, whose checkout has CRLF line endings (the section is now found and compared as text, and a rewrite keeps the file's own ending); the Repomix fallback said `yad setup` and `yad repo refresh` follow a team's skip — they do not, and now it says so; a tool yad cannot see (an older Spec Kit with only `.specify/`) would have been dropped — the skill's own check may now find it, never against a `skip`; with no Product found (a sibling repo, no workspace file) a skip was missed silently — the skill now says so; the old E84 "records" test passed on the section alone (it now looks outside it); the section writer skips fenced code; a second copy of the heading fails the test; leftover "not installed" wording now says "not used"; a connect skill's own line no longer says it follows the file it writes. **Review round 2 found, all fixed** (it approved, with suggestions): a skill whose tools are all connectors was told to ask `yad toolbox list` and then to ignore the answer — only a skill with a core tool asks now, and in `yad-ui` the rules say they are for the core tool; the docs said `yad repo refresh` writes `source: repomix` (it is `yad setup`, and refresh never changes `source`); the E84 records test could still pass on a skill's description (it now looks only at the body outside the section); the script rewrote only the skills a tool names, so it never took out a section the test refused — it now walks every skill, and refuses a rewrite that would remove more than the old section; the section finder now closes a fence only as CommonMark does, matches the heading without trailing spaces and ends the section at a `#` heading too; the Repomix line about `setup` and `refresh` moved from its fallback to its `note`, and each fallback sits on its own line. **Review round 3 found, all fixed:** round 2's guard against a rewrite that cuts too much refused every removal (a removal also takes the blank line around the section), and could not see a misread, because it measured with the same bounds it checked — it now reads the old section's text and refuses one holding a heading, a heading underline or a fence, which the toolbox never writes; the sync moved into `syncSkillFallbacks` so a test runs it in write mode (add, rewrite, remove, CRLF, refuse); a heading indented up to three spaces or with a tab now ends the section; the rules sentence names the tool ("For Spec Kit") instead of "the tool without a connector beside it" in a skill with no connector. **Review round 4 found, all fixed** (it approved, with suggestions): the guard refused a harmless `---` divider and said only "fix it by hand" — a `-` or `=` line now counts as a heading underline only right under a line of text, as CommonMark says, an HTML or quoted heading counts as a heading, and the message quotes the line and says to delete the old section and run the script again (`--check` names what a write would refuse); the finder and the test that counts copies spelled the heading differently (indent, case, closing `#`s), so a hand-written copy could stay beside a new one — one predicate, `isFallbackHeading`, now serves both; Impeccable's fallback starts "markdown-only" like the design connectors'. **Round 5 approved, with no bug**; its notes were done in round 6: the copy count reads fences as the finder does (`countFallbackHeadings`); a `---` under a list item or a heading is a divider, not an underline (round 6: only a real heading, and a list item that starts a list — `#hashtag` and `2. item` after a paragraph are text, so a `---` under them is still caught); the heading reader no longer slows on a long run of spaces or of `#`s (round 6: the first fix had only moved the slow case to `#`s); the Repomix row of the core table and the docs' "a turned-off plugin counts as missing" (it is `disabled`) now match; the backfill frontmatter example lost its `#` comment. **Review round 7 approved, and found the same slowness in four more patterns** (a lone `\r` or Unicode line break after a long run, and a long run of blank lines) **and one wrong exemption** (`2. a` / `3. b` under text, then `---`: the walk trusted any earlier item). Fixed, and one test now times every section reader on long, awkward inputs, so a slow pattern anywhere fails it — the slow cases had been found one per round. A list is now judged by its first item: a bullet or `1.` may follow text and start one, `2.` may not. **Review round 8 found, fixed:** round 7's one-pass list reading let a divider leave the list open, so a `Usage` / `---` heading after a list and a divider passed the guard — the unsafe direction. Rounds 5 to 8 had each found one more Markdown case by hand, so the guard is now checked against a real parser: `marked` (already installed by semantic-release) is a direct dev dependency, and a property test builds thousands of sections from 43 kinds of line and fails if the guard passes one in which marked sees a heading. Run wider (100,000 sections, 20 seeds) it found four places where marked reads differently from CommonMark — a `***` under text, a list ended by `#tag`, an indented item, an empty item; the guard now takes the reading that finds a heading, which may refuse a safe section (the fix is to delete it) but never passes an unsafe one. **Review round 9 found** more Markdown that the guard passed while marked saw a heading (a tab after a list marker, `01.`, an indented `---` inside a list). **Answered by a change of design, not a tenth patch:** the section now ends with its own marker line, `<!-- end: When a tool is missing -->`, and a rewrite replaces exactly the lines from the heading to the marker — so where the section ends is never read from the Markdown, and everything after the marker is outside it by construction. The guard (`sectionMisreadLine`, its list and divider rules) and the marked property test are gone, and `marked` is no longer a direct dev dependency; rounds 5 to 9's Markdown cases are moot. A section without its marker (made by hand, or written before it), a second copy, or a marker with no heading is refused (`skillSectionProblem`), never guessed at. **Review round 10 approved the marker design** — no input moves a rewrite outside the generator's own lines — and its notes were done: a code fence that never closes is refused (a write could otherwise append a copy it can never find again, on every run); YAML frontmatter is skipped when looking for where a new section goes; each refusal says what to delete or close; a removal takes its blank line only when one sits above it too. **Round 11 was clean**; its notes were done: the heading and marker are still looked for inside a frontmatter block (only where a new section goes skips it), so a copy there is never hidden; a removal takes its blank line also when a heading comes next, so insert-then-remove gives the file back exactly; the unclosed-fence refusal names the line; a byte-order mark before the frontmatter is allowed. **Round 12 found, fixed:** round 11's note let a heading-like YAML value (`  ## When a tool is missing` in a `description: |`) become the section's start, so with a stray marker in the body a write ran across the frontmatter's closing `---`. Now only the exact end marker is looked for inside frontmatter; a heading can only be found after it, so a section can never cross it — a file opening with a `---` divider around a section shows as a marker with no heading, and is refused. **Round 13 was clean**; its notes were done: frontmatter may close with `--- ` or `...`, as common readers accept, and a comment records that a copy without a marker inside frontmatter is read as metadata. **Round 14 found** that a file opening with a `---` divider was still read as frontmatter up to the next `---` or `...` (a `...` placeholder in a code sample counts), which could hide a section and let a write add a second copy. Fixed twice over: frontmatter now needs a YAML key on its second line (every SKILL.md opens `name:`), and a last check that ignores fences, frontmatter and indent refuses any write after which the file would hold more than one `##` heading line or marker line — so a copy the finder skipped (fenced, indented in a list) cannot be doubled. **Round 15 found, fixed:** that last check ran only on a write, so `--check` did not name what a write would refuse (it now runs in both modes); a quoted YAML key on the second line also opens frontmatter (a `# comment` does not, since `# T` is a title too). Left as known: a copy written in another heading form — in a `>` quote, underlined with `---`, as `<h2>` — is not counted; none can come from yad's own output. **Not here:** CodeRabbit and `gh` / `glab` (E130); a new record value for a skipped tool; `yad setup` and `yad repo refresh` following a Repomix `skip` (they still pack whenever `npx` is there). | M | E84 |
| E88 | Vet every default tool before it becomes a default — shipped as a **`vetted`** record on every toolbox entry, **`scripts/vet-check.mjs`** (release-check step 8/8) and **`REPOMIX_VERSION`**. **The row was a bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-10-02).** (1) **Every toolbox entry is vetted, all 13** — core, recommended pools and connectors — not only the three `yad setup` offers. (2) **A vetting record is licence + source only:** where the licence was read (`licenceFrom`: the source's own `LICENSE` file, or the vendor's terms page for a closed tool), the official `source`, the `release` tag on that day, and each registry `package` an install route names, confirmed to point back to the source, with its version. Not maintenance, not a security scan, not a tested version range — so `versions` stays null everywhere, and the comment that said E88 would earn the range now says it does not. (3) **Two gates:** a unit test fails while any entry's record is incomplete (`vettingProblems`), and the release check refuses to publish while any record is older than **180 days** (`staleVettings`, run by `scripts/vet-check.mjs`, which names each tool and the steps to re-vet). Age is checked only at release, against a day passed in, so `main` never turns red by itself. (4) **Repomix is pinned to its vetted npm version:** `yad setup` and `yad repo refresh` run `npx repomix@1.18.1`, not `@latest`, from one constant read off the record; the five skill files that write the command out as text say the same, and a test holds every `npx repomix@…` in `cli/`, `skills/` and `docs/CLI.md` to it. Moving up is a re-vet and a yadflow release. **Rules the row implied:** the pin reads a package VERSION, never a release tag (tags read `v1.18.1`, `skill-v4.5.0`, `cli-2.11.0`, and Impeccable's npm installer is 4.1.0 while its skill tag is 4.5.0); `on` is per entry, so re-vetting one tool re-dates one; a closed tool's proof must be a terms page, never a `LICENSE` path; every npm/PyPI package an install line names must be in the record, so an unvetted package cannot ride in on an install command; a team's own tool (`yad toolbox add --custom`) gets `vetted: null` — the team vets it. `yad toolbox list` prints `licence <x>, vetted <day>` under each shipped tool and `--json` carries the record (a key added; `jsonVersion` stays). **Review round 1 found, all fixed:** a `vetted.on` after today was never stale (the age went negative), so a typo of 2027 for 2026 kept a tool out of the gate for a year — the release check now refuses a future date too; `Date.parse` rolls an impossible day forward, so `2026-02-30` passed — a day must now come back out unchanged (`isCalendarDay`), and `vet-check --today` refuses one, where an invalid day had made every age NaN and passed everything; the printed global install `npm install -g repomix` took the newest release — now `@1.18.1`; the test reading install lines missed `npm install -g`, `npx -y`, `npm exec`, `uvx`, `pip3` and `--upgrade`, and the pin test missed a bare `npx repomix` (which runs the newest) — both widened, and the pin test now also reads `docs/WALKTHROUGH.md`, `TEAM-GUIDE.md` and `.csv` files, which turned up two more unpinned mentions. **Review round 2 found, all fixed:** the install-line test checked nothing on a form it could not read (`npm i -D`, `bunx`, `pnpm dlx`, `pip install "x>=1"`) — it now counts the installer words on each line and fails when one yields no package; the no-version ban did not cover `npm install -g repomix`, the line round 1 pinned; the docs-site report page still said `npx repomix`; and `vet-check` told a person with a future date to re-read a licence. **Review rounds 3 and 4 found, fixed:** a letter-case pattern for "is this a licence file" was wrong both ways — case-insensitive, it refused a closed tool's terms page at `/license-agreement`; upper case only, it missed a repository's `license` or `License.md` — so it is decided by where the URL points instead (`isRepoLicenceFile`: a `/blob/`, `/raw/` or `/src/` path, or raw.githubusercontent.com — so a repository's front page is not a file — then the file name in any case, decoded, query ignored; review 5); the installer count also reads `npm add`, `pnpm add` and `yarn add` / `yarn global add`, and the no-version ban reads `-D`, `--save-dev`, `-p` and the `--package=repomix` form. **What the vetting found (2026-10-02):** all ten GitHub sources active, none archived or a fork, every licence as the toolbox already said; BMAD's LICENSE is MIT with an attribution clause (GitHub reads NOASSERTION, npm says MIT); Figma's plugin comes from Anthropic's official marketplace pointing at `figma/mcp-server-guide`, which has **no LICENSE file**, so the proof is Figma's terms; Pencil now publishes as pen.dev (`pen.dev/terms-of-use`); PyPI's `specify-cli` and `deeptutor` list no project links and no provenance, so the link to the source is each project's own README naming the package. **Open question for later:** a closed tool that publishes its terms as a `LICENSE.md` in a public repository (Anthropic's `claude-code` does) would be refused today, because a closed tool's proof must be a terms page — decide that on purpose before such a tool is added. **Not here:** pinning the other `@latest` install lines (`@playwright/mcp@latest`), an expiry on `checked` (the install-route date), maintenance or vulnerability checks, and a tested version range. | S | — |
| E130 | The tools outside the toolbox. **Proposed 2026-10-01 by E87; not decided.** **Problem:** CodeRabbit (wired by `yad-engineer-review`, named by `yad-commit`, `yad-ship`, `yad-open-pr` and `yad-connect-repos`) and `gh` / `glab` (called by about eleven skills) are external tools with no toolbox entry, so no skill declares its fallback for them the E87 way. **Smallest increment:** a CodeRabbit entry, its install route checked against its own docs with a `checked` date (E84's rule), and the section in the skills that use it; `gh` / `glab` decided separately, because `yad doctor` already checks them. | S | E87 |
| E51 | Bind skills to steps, stored in the profile — shipped as **`profiles.<route>.steps`** in `.sdlc/skills.json` and **`--profile`** on `yad skill`. **The row was a bare title, so four decisions were the user's (2026-10-02).** (1) **Stored in the project's `.sdlc/skills.json`, under a new `profiles` key beside `steps`** — `"profiles": { "spike": { "steps": { "epic": "spike-skill" } } }`. Not in code: the engine ships no per-route defaults, so a route's line is always a team's choice. No new file and no shape change: E6 kept the wrapper object for exactly this, and `yad skill bind` already wrote back keys it did not know, so an older yadflow keeps the line and runs the project-wide answer for every route. (2) **The most specific line wins:** the epic's route, then the project-wide `steps`, then the catalogue. Inside each layer a renamed step still inherits its old id's line (`foundation` ← `discovery`), and the layer order comes first. The route is the one the epic RECORDS, as written (`recordedRoute`) — a route this release does not know included (rule 3), and never `epicProfileId`'s match from the chain: no recorded route, or no epic yet (`yad setup`, the first-epic hint), means the project-wide layer only. The Foundation hint on an empty Product uses the `foundation` route, the one `yad foundation new` seeds; a change epic uses the route it takes from its parent. (3) **`yad detect` is asked, never obeyed:** `yad skill bind` warns when a skill is not installed in the folder or the home folder and writes it anyway (a teammate may have it); `yad skill list` marks each skill `installed: true | false` (`?` in prose), with `installedChecked: false` and a warning when the home folder cannot be read. A plugin's skill counts as `<plugin>:<skill>`, the name an agent calls it by. "Found" is not "this agent will run it" — which copy an agent loads stays E50's open question. (4) **Every reader uses the epic's route:** `yad next` (the action, its printed line, the Build lanes, the re-opened lanes and the several-epics roll-up — one view per epic, built once in `cli/next.mjs` and handed to both the action and the line, so the JSON and the prose cannot disagree), `yad epic new` (the route just seeded, change-epics and the Foundation included), and `yad skill list --profile <p>`. **A fifth thing the readers forced:** the `yad-run` skill knows an epic, not a route, and a pre-shape-6 epic records none — so `yad skill list` also takes **`--epic <id>`**, which reads that epic's route the way `yad next` does, and `yad-run` reads that. **Rules the row implied:** a route's line on a Shape step the route never walks (`architecture` on `spike`) is refused, as a review gate is, because no epic would reach it; Build steps are on no route's row but run on every feature route, so they are allowed there and refused on the Product routes; an unknown route is recorded with a warning (rule 3, as an unknown step is); `--profile` has the step-id shape guard, which also stops `__proto__`; a `profiles` (or a route's entry, or its `steps`) that is there but not an object is refused, never written over; `unbind --profile` leaves the route's entry holding an empty `steps`, as E6 leaves the top level, and says which layer now runs the step. **`yad doctor`:** `skills:profiles` (fail) for a broken shape; `skills:profile-unusable`, `skills:unknown-profile`, `skills:profile-unknown-step`, `skills:profile-review-step` and `skills:off-route` (warn); `skills:bound` counts the route lines too. **`--json` (E1):** keys added, none renamed, so `jsonVersion` stays: `yad skill list` adds `profile`, `epic`, `installedChecked`, `profiles` and each row's `installed`, and `source` gains the value `profile`. **Left as it is (older than E51):** a change epic seeded off a parent that records no route takes the route `epicProfileId` matches from the parent's chain (E40's seeding rule), so a hand-written `[epic, epic-review]` parent gives a `chore` child, which then records `chore` and gets chore-only lines. A gate write on the parent stamps its route first, so this is rare. **Review round 1 found, all fixed:** the first cut used `epicProfileId`, which guesses from the chain — a pre-shape-6 `[epic, epic-review]` fits `chore`, so a chore-only line reached ordinary classic epics, and an epic recording a newer route got a different route's skill (the user's answer had said "recorded"); the empty-Product Foundation hint read the project-wide layer while `yad foundation new` read the route's; `yad doctor` called a route's old-name line (`discovery` on `foundation`) off-route though the resolver reads it; `unbind --profile` named the wrong layer for such a line; and the tests read the developer's real home folder. **Found on the way:** the several-epics roll-up of `yad next` never passed the bindings to its line at all, so its Build fallback named the catalogue's skills even for a project-wide binding; it now passes each epic's view. **Not here:** shipped per-route defaults; a route's own optional steps or other route settings (E54); recording which skill and version ran (E55). | M | E5 + E50 |

It is the hand-made list Part 7 says comes first; the orchestrator package (E92–E102), which ranks from measured evidence, stays separate and later. (2) **Versioned separately = its own `version` and `checked` day, apart from yadflow's package version**, printed with its age wherever the list is shown. (3) **A new read-only `yad skill recommend [<step>] [--profile <p> | --epic <id>] [--json]`**, and `yad skill list` names the picks (`recommended`) for a step still on the engine's default; `yad next` is unchanged. Each pick says whether this step already runs it and whether `yad detect` finds it installed here, and prints the bind command; `yad skill bind` stays the only writer. (4) **Every entry carries the E88 vetting record** (licence from the pool's LICENSE file, source, release tag, packages) plus `file`, its SKILL.md at that tag, and joins the 180-day release check (`scripts/vet-check.mjs`, step 8/8). **Rules the row implied:** **a suggestion is a chain, never a replacement** — the pool skill first and yadflow's own skill last (`yad skill bind architecture bmad-architecture yad-architecture`), because yadflow's skill writes the artifact where the gate looks, locks the contract and seeds the ledger, and the last output of a chain is the artifact (closed decision 7); a review gate is refused, as `bind` refuses one. A pick bound so that yadflow's skill does not run LAST (alone, `yad skill bind epic bmad-prd`, or before it) is allowed — the file wins — but never quiet: the row carries `ownSkillDropped` and a warning that nothing then files the artifact where the gate looks (rule 6). `yad skill list` prints the catalogue's version and age beside its hint. **What the check against the real repositories found (2026-10-03, every SKILL.md fetched at the release tag):** BMAD's familiar names `bmad-create-prd`, `bmad-create-architecture` and `bmad-dev-story` are deprecated forwarding skills, npx-only and removed in BMAD v7, so the list names `bmad-prd`, `bmad-architecture` and `bmad-build`; 14 mattpocock skills set `disable-model-invocation` (only a person can start them) and cannot be a chain step, so none is listed; `bmad-testarch-test-design` lives in a separate module repository outside the three pools and is left out; ECC's code review is an agent, not a skill; mattpocock `code-review` needs a setup only a person can run, and is left out; several ECC picks are only in its `developer` (or larger) install profile, and two skills write files of their own — each such pick carries a `caveat` the command prints. **Names are bound as each pool's vetted route installs them:** bare for BMAD and ECC (their npm installers), `mattpocock-skills:<name>` for mattpocock (a plugin only). `spec` and `tasks` have no pick: nothing in the pools fit well. 20 picks over 10 steps, catalogue version 1. | M | E51 |
| E131 | Team members — who is on the team, and every identity each person shows up under — shipped as **`cli/members.mjs`**, **`yad member add / list / remove`**, run by **`yad join`**, and the Product gate **`checks/member-check.mjs`**. **Proposed and decided by the user (2026-10-05)**, for two needs: E108's blocker (a), the git-name / platform-login double count, and a team list for the standup report (E132). **(1) One job: identity.** E62 removed the roster because it did six jobs; a member file does one — it says that some git emails and some platform accounts are one person. No role, no reviewer request, no allowlist: write access stays the only allowlist (E62 (3)). **(2) One file per person**, `.sdlc/members/<platform>-<login>.json`, named by their account on the Product's own platform and host (exactly one there), plus their other GitHub / GitLab / self-hosted accounts, the numeric account id, their git names and the date they joined. **One account and one email belong to one member:** the CI check refuses a second file holding someone's Product account or an email, and `yad doctor` reports one on disk; a shared one joins nothing until it is fixed (other accounts: see "who wins" in (7)). **(3) Proof, never a claim.** `yad member add` reads every account `gh` / `glab` is logged in to (logging the person in on the Product's platform when it can ask), and asks each with its OWN token for its VERIFIED emails (`user/emails`; GitHub needs `user:email`, a fine-grained token "Email addresses: read", GitLab `read_user`). An email joins when it is a verified email of the account AND the git `user.email` of the Product or of a registered repo on this machine. A second account joins only through a shared verified email. An account with none is named and left out. **(4) Emails are stored hashed** (`sha256:` of the trimmed, lower-cased address, unsalted — a known address can be checked against it; the proof commits carry each address as their author, as every commit does); a commit's email is hashed the same way on read. **Only the primary account's verified emails** (its own noreply address included — GitHub does not list it in `user/emails`, yet attributes its commits) go into the file: an email only another account verified would be attributed to nobody, and the PR would fail its own gate. **(5) The gate count can only JOIN, never remove.** `peopleEvidence` turns a commit whose email hash a member file pairs with a login into that login's commit — one person, not two. A person who never joined still counts; an unreadable file adds no pairing (the count stays as it was — over, not under: E71). **Only where the gate judged the file** (review 2): a verified GitHub Product whose `checks/member-check.mjs` is the shipped copy and is run by its product-checks workflow — never on a local-ledger or no-platform Product, and never while the gate or its workflow is outdated or edited (each file there is only its owner's statement); and only a file changed on origin's default branch (first-parent line) strictly after the last commit that changed the gate script OR its workflow in any way, and unchanged here since (review 4: one PR could disable the gate, add a forged file judged by its own disabled copy, and a later PR restore it — so any change to either resets trust, and each member runs `yad member add` again; a local commit or edit is never trusted). Everything is read from `origin/<default>` — the default branch as origin's own HEAD names it, or as origin's own copy of the settings on that branch names it (review 5: a local settings edit must not point the count elsewhere; review 6: when origin/HEAD and the settings disagree — a renamed default — nothing is trusted, and `yad member add` builds on the same name) — and each trusted file must be byte for byte on disk as there (an untracked or `--assume-unchanged` copy is not); a missing origin branch or a shallow clone trusts nothing (`yad doctor` `members:untrusted` names what is left out). `member-check` joins the required checks in docs/branch-protection.md; and only the account on the Product's own host: GitLab's API cannot tell CI which account wrote a commit, so on GitLab the pairings feed the team list only (the user's decision). E108's blocker (a) is therefore fixed for PROVEN pairs on GitHub only; a name typed two ways (`bo` / `Bo Chen`) still splits unless both commits carry an email the file holds. **(6) Written on a branch with its own PR/MR**, never in the checkout: one commit per email, each authored by that email, built with a private index on the default branch as origin has it (the `yad capture` way), signed when git signs, pushed to `yad/member/<login>`, PR/MR opened from the code task template. **This reverses E79's "join never commits, pushes or changes a shared file" for this one file** (the user's decision): join runs `yad member add` last, and any problem there is a warning plus the command to run later — never a failed join. **(7) The CI proof** (`checks/member-check.mjs`, on a verified Product's PRs; a Node script because it reads JSON and hashes, and both CI images carry Node): a PR may add or change only its author's own file, holding exactly one account on the Product's platform and host — the author's, with the author's numeric account id, so a renamed-and-reused login cannot take over an old file (the author comes from the event on GitHub, from the MR API on GitLab — which has no MR-author variable — and is never a bot); on GitHub EVERY email in a file it adds or changes must be the author email of a commit in the PR that GitHub attributes to that author (review 5: proving only new emails let an email that reached the branch while the gate was off ride along on any later edit; `yad member add` therefore re-proves every email on every run, keeping one only while the account still verifies it, and records the moment as `proved` — a time, so a same-day re-run still changes the file; review 6) (`commits/<sha>` → `author.login`; GitHub links a commit to an account only through an email the account verified); anyone may DELETE a file (removing a pairing can only raise the count); the reason goes in the commit by convention (`yad member remove <login> --reason`) — the gate does not check it. **Who wins:** an account on the Product's platform is proven, so it is its owner's — a second file naming it is refused by the gate and ignored on disk; an OTHER account two files claim is matched by nobody until one drops it, and is never refused (refusing it would let whoever claimed it first block its real owner). **No link is followed** (review 1): the gate refuses a `.sdlc` or `.sdlc/members` that is not a folder, a member entry that is not a plain file, and a case or Unicode twin of `.sdlc/members/` (review 2, the E115 and E121 lessons); `readMembers` refuses links too. The gate runs from the PR's own copy, as every Product gate does — branch protection is the backstop. **On GitLab with no API token the check FAILS** when a member file changed (fail closed, the user's decision). On GitLab a shared email is not refused (never proven, never counted); the CLI leaves a listed email out of the joiner's file with a warning instead of refusing the join (review 3). The check is new managed bytes: every wired Product reads `outdated` until `yad update`. On a local-ledger or no-platform Product there is no CI, so the pairing there is self-declared — `yad member list` and the docs say so. **(8) Status is derived on every read, never written, and nothing runs in the background.** Active = a commit or approval within the TTL (`members.ttlDays` in the Product settings, default 90 — E69's inactive hint); after it the platform is asked (once a day, cached in git's common folder) whether the account still has access to the Product's repo: yes is **idle**, a clear no is **left** (off the team list), anything else — a 403, an SSO org, offline — is **unknown**, never left. The next commit or approval makes a member active again with no re-join; the TTL never deletes a file, which would lose the pairing. **Not guaranteed:** that a person has no account they never added. **Shape:** new files only, under `.sdlc/members/`, stamped like every `.sdlc/` file; no state.json change, so no stamper. | M | E71 |
| E132 | Daily standup report — shipped as **`yad standup`** (`cli/standup.mjs`), with the platform list reader **`cli/pr-list.mjs`**. **Proposed by the user (2026-10-05); built after E131, whose team list it reads; two decisions by the user (2026-10-06).** Read-only, derived live, facts only — no score and no ranking (`yad usage`'s rule), and never an email, a commit message, a PR title or a comment body. Per member on E131's team list (active, idle and unknown; **left** listed once at the end): **done** since the last standup (commits, approvals, comments, engineer reviews of ships, steps closed — a merge is the merger's, anything else its `by` — and merged PRs/MRs), **working on now** (owned open steps E47, open claims E46, capture branches saved in the window E43, open PRs/MRs), and **waiting**. **(1) Waiting means both readings, each line saying what it waits for (the user's decision):** a gate on a step the member owns — the live E47 owner, else the epic's `owner` — waiting for approvals or for its review PR/MR to merge; a next move that is theirs (their owned author step is the epic's current step; the comments recorded in the gate's latest review round, without claiming which are answered); and their own open PRs/MRs whose checks fail or run or that have no approving review. E62 requests no reviewers, so no line says a review is waiting ON someone. An open gate on a step nobody owns is listed on its own. **(2) The platform is asked about the Product and every registered repo, by its `git_url`, cloned here or not (the user's decision):** one GraphQL call per GitHub repo (open PRs with review state and check rollup, and merged ones), two list calls per GitLab project (its `detailed_merge_status` printed as GitLab wrote it — only the two pipeline words become a check state, because `mergeable` on a project with no approval rule was approved by nobody). On GitHub a null `reviewDecision` (no required-review rule) is read from the latest opinionated reviews. A repo that cannot be read says "platform not read — <why>" (E70's rule: none only from an answer), a block with nothing in it says PRs may be missing, and a full page of 100 is said. **People seen but on no member file** get a section of their own (E71), and a record two members both match is given to neither. **Identity:** a commit by email hash or login (`gitAuthors` now also gives the commit's exact time, `at`, beside the UTC day the count reads); a ledger record by the login it records (the report reads the recorded name as a login; the gate count never does — E64); owners, claims and capture branches by `wipName` of each git name the file holds; a PR/MR by its author's login; robots (`[bot]`, and `yad-gate-sync` on a closing record) are left out. **Window:** midnight of the previous working day (Monday, Saturday and Sunday read back to Friday; no holiday logic) in one zone — UTC unless `--tz` — until now; `--since <date>|<N>h|<N>d` moves the start. Commits, claims and platform times are exact; a ledger record carries a UTC date only, so it is in when its date is on or after the UTC date of the start — included, never dropped; the header says both. **Status:** E131's, but an expired member's access is asked only when the Product's own platform answered, so a bad network never makes anyone `left`. One `git fetch` of the capture branches (shared with `yad claims` as `fetchCaptures`); E131's access cache is the only write. **Review 1:** the gate line reads the gate's own predicate (`gatePredicate`: engagement and revoked approvals), and says `yad gate advance` with no platform; a malformed ledger stops only its epic, said in "not read"; every file value is cleaned for the terminal and an email-shaped name is hidden; a monorepo's folders are one history, read once; a PR author is matched on the PR's platform and host; an empty section says what was not read. **Review 2:** error text is cleaned and names no path of this machine; an account two files claim matches neither on any platform; all three formats end with the same gaps (member files that could not be used, a failed fetch, a page that was full) and say the same thing when no member block prints; a clock that goes back at midnight starts the day at the first 00:00. **Review 3:** no path of this machine in any line (git's own reasons included; a relative `--dir` is never cut as text); a disputed account's records name the files that claim it; one login on two platforms stays two people seen; `--member` for someone who left says so; "no origin" is in every format. `--json` through E1's envelope; `--member`, `--me`, `--format md\|html`, `--out`; a foreign flag is refused, as `yad history` refuses one. No scheduler: a person runs it, or a CI schedule later; posting it somewhere is a later row. | M | E131 + E20 + E46 + E47 |

| E146 | **`YAD_PRODUCT_TOKEN` never goes quiet (issue #345).** The four Product-reading gates only defer when `checks/product-checkout.sh` cannot clone the Product. **The user's decisions (2026-10-19):** (1) a missing token on a PR/MR that is **not from a fork**, while the base record names the Product, is a loud `WARNING` (a `::warning` annotation on GitHub, a `WARNING` line everywhere) — **not a failure**; a fork's PR (never given secrets) and a run outside CI keep the note. GitHub's workflow passes `YAD_PR_FORK` (head repo ≠ this repo); on GitLab a pipeline outside the MR's target project is a fork's (`CI_PROJECT_ID` ≠ `CI_MERGE_REQUEST_PROJECT_ID`). (2) **CI reads the token's expiry**, where the token is: one API call with it (GitHub: the `github-authentication-token-expiration` header of `rate_limit`; GitLab: `/personal_access_tokens/self`), the token handed to `curl` in a header file; a `WARNING` within 30 days, a note beyond; an HTTP 401 turns a failed clone's FAIL into "expired or revoked" and a working clone's run into a `WARNING`. The API is chosen by the Product's host first, then by the CI. A clone-only GitLab token (`read_repository`) may not call the API (HTTP 403): a note pointing at doctor. Anything unreadable is a note: only the clone decides a failure. (3) **`yad doctor` checks where the token is set, by name only** (`cli/product-token.mjs`, the E70 read pattern, `YAD_PLATFORM_READ=0` off): an Actions or organisation secret on GitHub; a project or group CI/CD variable on GitLab, whose API also returns the VALUE — cut down to key, `protected` and `environment_scope` at the call. Warns: missing; a protected or environment-scoped GitLab variable (MR pipelines never get it); (4) a token set on a **GitLab group** — the "wrong level" the issue meant — naming the other registered repos in that group. A failed read is "not known", a warning, never "missing"; a repo checked out locally is checked only when it carries the product-link record (on disk or on its default branch); one known only by its URL is always checked. On a GitLab Product it also warns about its own project access tokens that can clone it (`read_repository` or `api`) and expire within 30 days or expired in the last 30 — whatever platform the code repos are on — saying it cannot tell which one a variable holds. An issue comment said an expired token skips quietly; it did not — a token set and a clone that fails was already a FAIL (E120) — what was missing was the reason. **Costs:** `product-checkout.sh` and the GitHub `yad-checks.yml` change wired bytes, so every wired code repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`. **Not here:** GitHub has no way to read a secret's value or its token's expiry from outside a workflow, so doctor cannot warn ahead on GitHub; the CI warning is that warning. **Shipped (#372).** | S | E120 |
| E147 | **The pr-template gate reads the whole GitLab description (issue #343).** GitLab hands CI only the first 2700 characters of an MR description, so a valid one whose required sections sat further down failed (#164 only named the cut). Now, in GitLab CI, a description that reached the cut is read again in full from the merge request API and that is what the gate checks — with the `GITLAB_TOKEN`/`SDLC_API_TOKEN` CI/CD variable `yad-verified-commits` already needs, else the job's own `CI_JOB_TOKEN` (GitLab's job-token access list allows a single merge request; an older GitLab may not). The cut is known from GitLab's `CI_MERGE_REQUEST_DESCRIPTION_IS_TRUNCATED`, else from the length. A fork's pipeline cannot read the target MR, and its note says so instead of asking for a variable. The token goes to `curl` in a header file and only to `$CI_API_V4_URL`; `node` (in every yad job image) decodes the JSON. The full text is checked, not trusted: a section it lacks still fails. Anything unreadable falls back to the cut body, and the #164 note then also says why, with advice only where a token change helps (set `GITLAB_TOKEN`; renew, scope or replace a refused one; a fork cannot read the target). Only bodies at the cut make the call. **Costs:** `pr-template.sh` changes wired bytes (code repos and the Product): `outdated` until `yad update`. **Shipped (#377).** | S | — |
| E148 | **One page for every CI gate, and messages that say what to change (issue #351).** `docs/GATES.md` lists every gate — where it runs (GitHub job, GitLab job, code repo or Product), what it checks, its exemptions (the `ci`/`chore`/`build`/`test` types that need no `Task:` trailer, merge commits, forks, a Product CI cannot reach), what it reads, and every failure message with what to change. Linked from the README, `docs/CLI.md`, both sites and `check-gates.md` (which stays the skill's reference for how and why). An audit counted 214 failure messages, 90 of which said what was wrong but not what to do. **The user's decision (2026-10-19):** this row fixes the texts copied into many gates and the five worst; the rest go to follow-up issues, one per gate. Fixed here: base ref not found, no merge base and merge-tree failed (11 gates, member-check included) now name `fetch-depth: 0` / `GIT_DEPTH: "0"`; the five vague "where the Product is read from" texts in all four Product-reading gates; build/test/lint names the failing step and the missing-script case (it ended with npm's output only); spec-link's missing `Task:` trailer and missing `link.md`; verified-commits tells "could not ask GitHub" from "unsigned" and says how to re-sign; epic-open's one text for two causes (a bad `epic:`, an empty `product-repo`) split; the orphan story link (3 gates), orphan slice, missing `parent:` and orphan thread; ledger-guard says a revert commit does not help. Each new clause is asserted, and a text copied into several gates is run in every copy. **Costs:** 11 wired gate scripts change (`spec-link`, `contract-check`, `build-test-lint`, `lineage-check`, `epic-open`, `reconcile-debt-check`, `commit-message`, `verified-commits`, `ledger-guard`, `product-checkout`, `member-check.mjs`; `backfill-check.sh` is placed by hand), so every wired repo reads `outdated` until `yad update`. **Not here:** `::error` annotations on GitHub (no gate prints one today). **Shipped (#379).** | S | — |

It runs the repo's **own installed `checks/`** against `origin/<default>` (fetched; `--base`, `--no-fetch`), names a gate that differs from the shipped one, runs every gate even after one fails, and exits 1 on any FAIL; `--json` lists each gate's status and FAIL lines. Run from the Product it runs the Product's gates with the branch and changed paths CI passes. **Not the same as CI, said in the output:** signatures are checked with git — the Verified badge exists only after the push; `member-check` is left to CI when a member file changes (it needs the PR's author); the PR text is given by hand (a skipped gate is not a pass); the build gates use this machine's tools; CI on GitHub tests the merge, this run the branch tip. The GitLab 2700-character cut is a warning. **Costs:** no gate script or CI file changes; the `yad-checks` and `yad-implement` skill copies read `outdated` until `yad update`. **Shipped (#387).** | M | E120, E148 |
| E150 | **`yad build start\|step\|trust\|ship\|done`: one command per Build record (issue #346).** The four Build records — a story's lane, a trust run, a ship, the story's `shipped` status — were written by hand by the skills, each time looking up the format; three "shipped" signals (the status line, the ship records, every lane step done) could disagree. **The user's decisions (2026-10-19):** (1) one family, `yad build …`, on the Product (not `yad ship`, which commits and opens a PR in a code repo); (2) `build ship` reads the merge commit, branch, passing checks, approvers (with the engagement signal), the body's risk level and the merge date from the **merged** PR/MR, in the repo's own checkout, and refuses an unmerged PR or one from another task's branch — `--merge-commit`/`--approver` only where the platform cannot be read; (3) `build done` makes the three signals agree: every task in each repo's `tasks.md` on its default branch has a ship, every earlier lane step is done, then `engineer-review` is closed in each lane and the status set to `shipped` (lanes first, status last, rolled back on failure); (4) one PR; (5, 2026-10-20) a story's tasks each walk `implement` and `checks`: `--task <T>` reopens them for the next task once the lane is at `engineer-review`, each close kept per task, so the review finalizes the right task's run; (6, 2026-10-20, revised the same day after review rounds 5–7 kept finding holes in reading commit text) a task merged with no PR is recorded from its commit (`--merge-commit`, no `--pr`), which must be on the default branch and name the task, **and the platform is asked which PR/MR merged it**: a PR → refused naming `--pr N`; no answer → refused; none → `pr: "none"`; where the platform cannot be read the person vouches (the commit may not name a different task). When in doubt it refuses and writes nothing: a lane is walked in order; the review step closes only through `done`; `done` refuses a repo with no lane or an empty one, a stray lane, a blocked review, a failed fetch, a story owing no repo, an epic not in Build, two status lines, or a story file not named `<story>.md` (tidy and the seal read stories by file name). The records keep the shapes the readers expect; the skills (yad-run, yad-spec, yad-implement, yad-engineer-review, yad-reconcile) now run the commands. New error code `YAD-STATE-009`. **Costs:** no gate script or CI file changes; seven skill folders change (yad-run, yad-spec, yad-implement, yad-engineer-review, yad-reconcile, yad-epic, yad-stories), so their copies read `outdated` until `yad update`. **Not here:** the automatic ship record on merge (#355). **Shipped (#388).** | M | E145 |
| E151 | **`yad build merged`: ask the platform whether a task's PR/MR merged (issue #355).** The agent waited for a person to say "done, merged" before it recorded the ship and went on. **The user's decisions (2026-10-20):** (1) a new `yad build merged <story> --task <T> --repo <r>` answers one `state` — `shipped` (a ship is already recorded; the platform is not asked), `merged` (with the merge commit), `open`, `closed` (without a merge) or `none` — and `--ship` records the ship once merged by running `yad build ship` for it, never a second write path; (2) the PR/MR is found by the task id in its source branch, among the repo's newest 100 (`partial` when the page was full), with `--pr <n>` still accepted; two PRs/MRs that are not closed carrying one task are refused, naming them; (3) one check per run, nothing waits: the agent runs it again when it resumes. ## Wave 5 — The missing third

| ID | Task | Size | Needs |
|---|---|---|---|
| E32 | Release phase | L | E22 |
| E33 | Operate phase | L | E32 |
| E133 | A branching strategy for each repo. **Proposed by the user (2026-10-10); not decided.** **The rule:** the Product repo is always **trunk-based** — one default branch, short-lived review branches (`review/<epic>/<artifact>`) merged back through the gate — and that is fixed, not a choice. Each **code repo** uses the strategy its team prefers for that project: trunk-based (the default), GitHub Flow, GitFlow, GitLab Flow (environment branches) or release branches. **Problem:** today every code repo is treated as trunk-based without saying so — `yad-implement` branches from the repo's default branch, `yad open-pr` targets the platform's default branch unless `--base` is typed each time, and `yad-sync-repos` pulls the registry's `default_branch` — so a team on GitFlow (work merges into `develop`, `main` holds releases only) gets task branches cut from, and PRs aimed at, the wrong branch. **Proposal:** record the choice per repo in `.sdlc/repos.json` (asked at `yad setup` and `yad-connect-repos`, trunk-based when unset), and have the readers follow it: the branch a task starts from and its PR targets, the branch sync pulls, the branches the gates run on, and the release and hotfix branch names. `yad doctor` warns when a repo's platform settings disagree with its recorded strategy. **Open questions:** whether feature flags are named for trunk-based repos; how a hotfix under GitFlow threads into `yad-change`; whether forking is in scope. | M | — |

## The estimate — Claude Code implementing, a human reviewing

Claude Code changes the ratio **per kind of task**, not uniformly:

| Kind of task | Speed-up | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Wide mechanical sweeps — E29, E56, E62, E104, E13 | 8–10× | Hundreds of places, test-guarded, no design |
| New commands with clear specs — migrate, skip, defer, history, join | 4–5× | The spec is in this doc; the tests are the spec |
| Design-heavy engine work — E4, E5, E17, E1 | 2–3× | Typing is fast; deciding is not |
| Integration with real external tools — E23, E25, E8, E50 | 1.5–2× | Each adapter is tested against a real agent CLI by a human watching |

| Scope | Tasks | Working days |
|---|---|---|
| **Full plan** | 102 (counted before E126–E144 were added) | **~170 optimistic · ~250 realistic** (about a year) |
| **v4.0 cut line** | ~39 | **~60 optimistic · ~90 realistic** (three to four months) |

Assumes one person reviewing and deciding 2–3 hours a day. The bottleneck is not
typing. It is: reviewing ~100 pull requests, clearing the open decisions (each one
blocks a task), a human watching each agent adapter run once, and rework rounds.

## The v4.0 cut line

Not the whole plan — the smallest set that is honestly sellable and that lets the
app start being designed the day it lands.

> **v4.0 = Wave 0 + Wave 1 + Wave 2 + E62 + E64 + E1 + E122 + E123 + E124**

That is: the vocabulary settled, the engine owning the lifecycle, the roster gone,
approvals recorded as evidence, every command speaking JSON, and one word — Product —
where users used to meet `hub` (added 2026-09-28: renaming what users see is a breaking
change, and making it before 4.0 reaches `latest` means users break once, not again in
v5). Everything else is v4.x or v5.

## Where the risk sits

| Item | Risk |
|---|---|
| **E17** | Everything visual depends on it. The builder cannot exist until the engine, not a skill, owns the lifecycle |
| **E1** | Large, dull, touches every command. Easy to under-estimate |
| **E25** | Every AI tool behaves differently. The fallback path is what saves us |
| **E62** | 399 references across 20 files, plus 126 tests. Deletion, but wide |
| **E17b** | The skills stop hand-writing `state.json`. Easy to forget, and if forgotten the engine and the skills fight over one file |

# Part 7 — The skill orchestrator (companion package)

A **separate package**, built after Wave 2. Yadflow uses it; it does not need
yadflow.

## The problem it solves

People now install skill collections holding dozens of skills each. Install three
and the agent is choosing between several hundred descriptions, which makes
selection worse, not better.

The value is **not** "we pick a better skill than the model can."

> **Narrow several hundred candidates down to three good ones, with the reasons
> shown, so the model chooses well and cheaply.**

## Why a separate package

| Factor | Answer |
|---|---|
| **More users** | Its audience is every Claude Code / Codex / Cursor user, not only teams who want a governed lifecycle. It is useful in 30 seconds with no process change. Yadflow has 0 stars and 0 dependents; this is the cheapest way to fix that |
| **Accuracy** | Accuracy comes from **how rich the input is**, not from where the code lives. Yadflow's context builder (E24) produces a full brief — goal, inputs, output shape, done-rule, constraints, budget — so a separate package is just as accurate |

## The boundary

| Side | Owns | Knows nothing about |
|---|---|---|
| **Orchestrator** | Finding skills · reading metadata · deduplicating · ranking · cost estimates · a shortlist **with reasons** | SDLC, phases, gates, epics |
| **Yadflow** | Steps, phases, gates, Foundation, repo map · **building the brief** · what to do with the shortlist | How skills are ranked |

**The orchestrator recommends only. It never runs anything.** Running belongs to the
session runner (E25).

## Positioning: lead with cost, not quality

| Claim | Verdict |
|---|---|
| "We pick the best skill" | Unprovable, invites argument |
| "This took 8,000 tokens instead of 60,000" | **Measurable. Nobody argues with a number** |

Cost lines up with everything else here — the three-level context, the precise
prompt builder, local checks that save CI minutes.

## Where the evidence comes from

**The audit trail is the training data.** Nothing extra to instrument.

Quality is measured as **rework distance** — how much the human changed it — never
by asking anyone to rate anything.

| Outcome | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Approved, no edits | Excellent |
| Approved, small edits | Good |
| Approved after heavy rewriting | Poor — cost more than it saved |
| Rejected at the gate | Bad fit |
| **Regenerated without review** | The strongest negative signal of all |

Each run records **shape only** — step kind, skill, version, cost, outcome, rework
lines, brief size. No content, no names, no repo, no code.

## Three stages of evidence

| Stage | Source | Available |
|---|---|---|
| **1 — Static fit** | Does the skill's output match the step? Does it need a missing tool? How big is it? | **Day one, zero data.** This alone cuts 300 candidates to about 10 — which was the whole point |
| **2 — Our benchmark** | We run fixed briefs against popular skills on our machines, at our cost, and ship the results | Shipped with the package. A download, never an upload |
| **3 — Their history** | The user's own runs, on their stack, judged by their reviewers | Grows over time, **overrides both** |

> **Ours is the starting point. Theirs is the truth.**

## Three tiers of user — and the funnel

Outside yadflow the ranker still learns, because **a commit is a gate**:
commit-or-discard, edit distance before committing, and regeneration are all
visible in plain git.

| Tier | Evidence | Quality |
|---|---|---|
| No git | Static fit + benchmark | Useful, never improves |
| **Git, no yadflow** | + commit-derived rework | Improves on its own. Noisy |
| **Yadflow** | + gate-verified rework | Clean — a named human approved it |

> **Yadflow makes the ranker better by giving it a truer signal, not by withholding
> features.** An honest funnel: the free tool is genuinely good, the paid path is
> genuinely better, and the reason is true.

## Keeping the benchmark honest

- Ships as a **separately versioned data file** — refreshes without a package release
- **Re-runs when a skill publishes a new version**, not on a blind schedule (cheaper
  and more accurate — an unchanged skill needs no re-measuring)
- **Always states its own age** and flags skills that have moved since
- **The method and briefs are published** so anyone can re-run and check them
- Running it is a real recurring cost, paid by us in tokens. Tier it: popular skills
  often, the long tail rarely

## Four traps

| Trap | Guard |
|---|---|
| Small numbers lie | No recommendation below a minimum run count. *"Not enough data yet"* is a valid, trust-building answer |
| Confounding — a skill drew the hard steps | Only compare within the same step kind |
| Our benchmark will favour our own skills | Publish the method. A benchmark nobody can check is marketing |
| Rework may be the brief's fault, not the skill's | Record brief size too, so a bad brief shows up across every skill |

## Risks

| Risk | Response |
|---|---|
| Skill descriptions are marketing copy | Rank on measured evidence, not only on descriptions |
| No ground truth for "best" | Which is why cost is the headline |
| Built on someone else's moving surface | Keep the adapter thin; assume it breaks yearly |
| Two products, one small team | Sequence them. Never run both roadmaps at once |
| A harness vendor builds it in | **Cross-harness neutrality is the moat.** Claude Code will never rank Codex's skills |

## Tasks — after Wave 2

E50, E51 and E55 are the manual version of this. They ship first and gather the
evidence; the ranker is built on that data, never before it.

| ID | Task | Size | Needs |
|---|---|---|---|
| E89 | Define the brief format — the contract between engine and orchestrator | S | E24 |
| E90 | Yadflow emits a brief per step | S | E89 + E24 |
| E91 | Per-run record: step kind, skill, version, cost, outcome, rework lines, brief size | M | E55 + E44 |
| E95 | Rework measurement — diff the agent commit against the approved version | S | E44 |
| E96 | Cost capture — read agent-reported usage, or measure the prompt we built | S | E25 |
| E99 | Local evidence store and roll-up per step kind, with a minimum-count guard | S | E91 |
| E92 | *(new package)* Skill index — find, read, deduplicate across harnesses | M | — |
| E97 | *(new package)* Static fit scoring — works with zero evidence | M | E92 |
| E100 | *(new package)* Git-only evidence — commit vs discard, edit distance, regeneration. **Needs a harness hook** to record which skill ran, one per harness like E23 | L | E92 |
| E101 | *(new package)* Evidence tiering — use the best signal available, and say which | S | E100 + E99 |
| E98 | *(new package)* Benchmark harness — fixed briefs, popular skills, published results | M | E92 |
| E102 | *(new package)* Benchmark distribution — versioned file, refreshes without a release | S | E98 |
| E103 | *(new package)* Version-triggered benchmark re-runs | S | E98 |
| E93 | *(new package)* Ranker — shortlist with reasons and a cost estimate | L | E92 + E91 |
| E94 | *(new package)* CLI and library surface | M | E93 |

---

# Part 8 — Rules that never bend

Everything in this plan is negotiable except these.

1. **A review gate can never be `auto`.** A human clears every gate. This is what
   the product *is*.
2. **A review gate cannot be skipped on its own.** Skipping a step skips its gate
   with it; you cannot keep the step and drop the approval.
3. **An unattended agent may write artifacts and code. It may never pass a gate.**
4. **A generated skill must be approved by a human before it is bound.** We sell
   governance; we cannot let an AI write its own instructions unwatched.
5. **You may skip authoring a contract. You may never skip having one.**
6. **The engine never goes quiet about what is unprotected.**
7. **There is always an escape hatch, and it is always recorded.**
8. **One privacy promise, across every product we ship: nothing ever leaves your
   machine.** No telemetry, no opt-in sharing, no exceptions. A sentence you can
   say in four seconds and never have to qualify is worth more than better data.
9. **The ledger is written by one trusted identity only.** In `verified` mode that
   is the CI bot with a platform-Verified signature. If that ever changes, it
   changes by design, not by accident.
10. **A release is a human decision.** Nothing reaches `yadflow@latest` unless a
    person fast-forwards `release` after the release check passes. A robot may
    build, test and tag; it may not decide that users get a new version.

---

# Part 9 — Closed decisions

Every question that was open when this plan was written, and how it was closed.
There are no open questions left.

| # | Question | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which engine features stay free? | **All of them.** The app is the only paid thing | MIT cannot hide features. A crippled free tier makes people distrust a governance tool. The multi-project view is not withheld — it simply does not belong in a per-project CLI |
| 2 | Does MIT stay on the engine? | **Yes, permanently.** Say so in the README | The engine is not the moat; the control room, the benchmark data and the cross-harness orchestrator are — and all three are work, not secrets. Companies that tightened an open licence lost trust and got forked within weeks |
| 3 | How does a serverless app check who paid? | **A signed offline licence file.** See below | Matches the privacy promise: not even a licence check leaves the machine |
| 4 | Price and plan shape | **Per person, per month, one plan.** No per-project pricing. The number is set after the first ten real conversations with users | Charging per project punishes the exact behaviour we sell. There is no willingness-to-pay data yet, and a guessed number is worse than a delayed one. Price it as a professional tool, not a consumer app |
| 5 | How do we get the first users? | **Orchestrator-first funnel.** Ship E29, then the free orchestrator, which mentions yadflow once and quietly. Talk to every early user personally | A tiny tool useful in 30 seconds reaches a hundred times more people than a governed lifecycle does. The one engaged contributor is worth more than a thousand stars |
| 6 | Do we ever measure adoption? | **Never.** Public signals only — npm, GitHub issues, stars | Closed by rule 8. A floor is enough to know whether we are growing |
| 7 | Chain or panel for multi-skill steps? | **Chain only.** One skill per step by default; extras opt-in with a cost warning. Panel deferred, maybe forever | Chain has a clear winner (the last output). Panel needs a picking step — a human reading three architectures, or an AI judge nobody should trust in a governance tool |
| 8 | The engine/app boundary | **`--json` (E1) and MCP (E2). Nothing else.** No private API | If the app uses the same door as everyone else, the door stays honest and we feel every gap ourselves. Anyone may build a competing app on the engine — that is what MIT means |

## The licence check, in detail

**Signed offline file. No hardware lock. Read-only on expiry, never locked.**

| Layer | How | Why it works |
|---|---|---|
| **The name on the file** | The file carries the buyer's name and email, signed with our key. The app shows it always: *"Licensed to Alice Chen · 5 seats · until 2027-09"* | Sharing the file means sharing your name. This is the Sublime Text model, and it deters better than any hardware lock with zero false positives and zero support tickets |
| **Seats, shown not enforced** | The file says `seats: 5`. The ledger already records every approver. `yad doctor` warns *on their own machine* when the ledger shows more distinct people than the licence covers | Companies fix this themselves because their own compliance team sees it. A warning inside their own audit trail beats a lock, and nothing leaves the machine |
| **Short renewals** | A 12-month file. A copied file expires like any other | The natural limit |
| **Read-only on expiry** | The app keeps working for reading; editing asks you to renew | A pirate with an expired copy sees a helpful tool that asks nicely — some convert. A locked app converts nobody. Locking anyone out of their own audit trail would end our reputation in one post |

**What we deliberately do not do, and why:**

| Not this | Because |
|---|---|
| MAC address or any hardware fingerprint | A MAC belongs to a network card, not a machine — laptops have several, macOS and Windows randomise it, VMs fake it, and it is changed with one command. It fails only the honest, and it would require a device identifier to leave the machine at purchase, breaking rule 8 at the exact moment the customer decides whether to trust us |
| Online activation or phone-home | Needs a server. Breaks the offline promise and rule 8 |
| Locking the app | See above |

Piracy of professional B2B tools is small and is not stopped by DRM. Companies pay
for the invoice, the compliance record and the support contact — none of which come
with a copied file. The goal is not to make copying impossible; it is to make paying
the obvious, respectable choice and copying slightly embarrassing.

---

# Appendix — What got smaller

Four decisions in this session **removed** work rather than adding it:

| Decision | Effect |
|---|---|
| Remove earned automation | Deletes trust thresholds, earn checks, nudge reporting |
| `required` moves to the profile | Deletes `SKIPPABLE_STEPS`. Shipped as `optional` on the profile row (E35), not a new field |
| Read CODEOWNERS, do not duplicate | Deletes `repos.json` `domain_owners` and its drift check |
| **Remove the roster entirely** | Deletes `roster.mjs`, the setup section, the sync logic, and the allowlist half of `verified-commits.sh` |

And two decisions removed the need for new work: the `driver`/`advance` dials
already covered the "who contributes" model, and Foundation reuses one artifact
for both greenfield and brownfield.

**The engine gets simpler and more capable at the same time.** That is the sign
this plan is going the right way.
